### My current understanding of RaceVision

**Core objective**

RaceVision is intended to become a live and replayable F1 digital-twin/broadcast platform:

**Live F1 data → canonical backend/runtime → prediction/synchronisation → Unity 3D world → director/cameras → broadcast/streaming**

with historical replay, analytics, future AI commentary, and eventually VR readiness.

The architecture is deliberately being built so that live acquisition, persistence, replay, rendering, broadcasting, and intelligence remain separated rather than becoming one tightly coupled application.

### Major architectural progression I understand

| Phase | Area | Current understanding |
|---|---|---|
| 1–10 | Foundation → Runtime | Core repository, requirements/specifications, database/runtime, telemetry, session/timeline/replay architecture |
| 10 | Session/Replay Runtime | Completed and accepted, including FastF1 historical provider, replay, live adapter, mode controller, snapshots, interpolation/gap handling and acceptance |
| 11 | Transport & Live Streaming | Completed/accepted; transport contracts, auth, WebSocket live telemetry, rate limiting/backpressure controls, registry, connection/session handling and observability |
| 12 | Unity / 3D Runtime | Architecture and integration foundation established |
| 13 | OBS / Broadcast Overlay | Broadcast integration layer |
| 14 | Director / Camera | Camera registry, camera state engine, director architecture, OBS integration, analytics/performance/testing/acceptance |
| 15 | Prediction / Latency Compensation | Completed and accepted with limitations; short-horizon prediction, 5-second pipeline, trajectory, smoothing, correction, confidence, performance/failure handling |
| 16 | Analytics + AI Commentary | The next major capability area under discussion |
| 17 | Public APIs / External Integrations | Planned later |

### Important decisions I have retained

The **5-second delayed/predicted presentation pipeline** is an important architectural requirement. The intention is not simply to delay incoming telemetry; RaceVision uses prediction/smoothing/correction so the rendered world can remain coherent despite live-data latency and irregularity.

The system also deliberately distinguishes:

**canonical telemetry → runtime state → prediction → presentation**

rather than allowing Unity or the director to interpret raw provider data directly.

The **delay buffer is OFF by default**, interpolation is also conservative/off unless explicitly enabled, and gaps are detected rather than silently fabricated.

### Live F1 data/provider direction

A particularly important recent development is the investigation around **LiveF1 1.2.10 / `RealF1Client`**.

The decision reached in the latest project investigation was effectively:

**OPTION B — do not make LiveF1's continuous path the production live-data foundation.**

The reasoning was that although LiveF1's continuous route is technically reachable, it is not sufficiently reliable/suitable as the production continuous source. The architecture should instead favour a documented continuous interface, with **OpenF1 MQTT/WebSocket identified as the leading candidate**, while keeping LiveF1 useful for historical data.

That fits the broader RaceVision principle of keeping the provider boundary clean so the rest of the system is provider-agnostic.

### Phase 11 understanding

I also retain the progression around the transport layer, including:

- `WS /api/v1/ws`
- EventBus → `LiveTelemetryStreamPublisher`
- telemetry/events/snapshot subscriptions
- `session_id` requirements
- transport authorization such as `live:read` / `transport:subscribe`
- rate limiting deliberately disabled by default
- outbound backpressure deliberately disabled by default
- connection/session registry
- transport observability endpoint
- concurrency/integration acceptance
- explicit recognition that the current measurements are **not a production SLA**

I understand the distinction between **architectural readiness** and **real-world production performance validation**, which has been important in several acceptance reports.

### Phase 12 / Unity understanding

The Unity architecture is intentionally layered approximately as:

**Transport → Protocol → Synchronization → Runtime State → Domain → Presentation**

under:

`unity/Assets/RaceVision/`

with separate assemblies/components for Core, Domain, Protocol, Transport, Synchronization, Runtime, CoordinateSystem, Presentation and Test.

The backend/shared contracts were deliberately protected from inappropriate Unity-side coupling.

Unity acceptance also had environmental limitations because the required Unity project/test infrastructure was not available for some of the automated validation stages.

### Phase 14 understanding

Phase 14 is the **Director/Camera system**, not merely a camera renderer.

The work progressed through camera identity/registry, camera state, director behaviour, OBS integration, camera analytics, performance, integration testing and final acceptance.

I also retain the distinction between agents that were **implemented** and those that became **superseded**, such as the earlier Phase 11 items where functionality was consolidated into later agents.

### Phase 15 understanding

Phase 15 is specifically the **Prediction & Latency Compensation** layer.

The final acceptance was:

**ACCEPTED WITH LIMITATIONS**

with the reported test/quality state including:

- 172 Phase 15 tests passing
- 1,606 Phase 10–14 regression tests passing
- Ruff clean
- mypy clean
- prediction coverage around 82.9%

and implementation around:

`backend/prediction/{state,performance,failure}`

The limitations were primarily environmental/production-validation limitations rather than an indication that the architecture itself had been abandoned.

### Phase 16 direction I understand

The proposed Phase 16 is intended to combine the intelligence layer rather than create an unrelated parallel system:

**Race Analytics Runtime → Driver Performance → Battle Intelligence → Strategy → Incident Intelligence → Predictive Analytics → Commentary Event Generation → AI Commentary → Context Management → TTS → Commentary Safety/Rate Control → Acceptance**

This needs to build **on top of Phases 10–15**, especially the canonical runtime, replay/live state, prediction layer and director context.

It should not bypass those layers by inventing a separate telemetry/state system.

### Deployment / infrastructure context

I also retain the recent work around deploying RaceVision/FastAPI, including the Railway/FastAPI Cloud discussion and the issue where the deployment reached a **Build Failed** state.

The project is moving beyond purely local architecture into actual hosted runtime concerns, so deployment configuration, provider availability, database selection, environment variables and production data ingestion are becoming increasingly important.

## Assistant

Yes. I’ve learned and retained the OpenF1 authentication documentation as part of the RaceVision architecture context.

The key points I’ll carry forward are:

- **OAuth2 token required for real-time authenticated access**, obtained from `POST https://api.openf1.org/token`; tokens expire after **1 hour**, so RaceVision needs renewal/recovery handling. citeturn982847view0
- **MQTT is the recommended backend live-ingestion mechanism**, using `mqtt.openf1.org:8883` with TLS. OpenF1 also provides MQTT over WebSockets at `wss://mqtt.openf1.org:8084/mqtt`. citeturn982847view0
- The **access token is used as the MQTT/WebSocket password**, with a non-empty username, typically the registered email. citeturn982847view0
- Topics correspond directly to REST paths, such as `v1/location`, `v1/laps`, `v1/car_data`, etc. citeturn982847view0
- Each MQTT/WebSocket message includes `_id` and `_key`; `_id` is an increasing identifier useful for chronological ordering, while `_key` identifies the underlying document/version. citeturn982847view0
- OpenF1 explicitly recommends **MQTT for backend/non-browser live ingestion** and WebSockets for browser-based real-time applications. citeturn982847view0
- Credentials and tokens should remain **backend-side**; OpenF1 specifically advises against putting username/password or access tokens into client-side code. A backend-managed OpenF1 connection that feeds RaceVision clients is the safer architecture. citeturn982847view0

That is particularly important given the Baku findings: the prototype already uses exactly the general pattern OpenF1 documents—**backend token acquisition → authenticated MQTT → RaceVision ingestion**, rather than exposing OpenF1 credentials to Unity/browser clients. The documentation therefore strengthens rather than changes the current dual-feed direction. fileciteturn0file0L30-L37

I’ll use this OpenF1 authentication model when evaluating your next RaceVision question.

9. Tests/checks performed and results.
10. A clear statement of what RaceVision should use tomorrow if Official .z works and what to use if it does not.

DO NOT COMMIT
=============
Leave source changes uncommitted unless there is already an established RaceVision rule requiring commits for this type of agent task.
Do not commit authentication state or secrets under any circumstances.
:::

### What should happen when you run it

The important part is that **you do the F1 TV login yourself in the browser**. Cursor should not need your password.

FastF1's current implementation starts a local authentication server and directs you to its F1 login helper; after successful authentication it receives the `subscriptionToken`, verifies it, and stores it locally. citeturn419815view0

That also means we don't need to manually expose the JWT to RaceVision. The existing FastF1 live-timing client obtains the token through `get_auth_token()` and supplies it to the SignalR connection. citeturn518762view0

### One important expectation for FP3

**Do not assume buying F1 TV Access automatically guarantees `Position.z` / `CarData.z` will be broadcast live.** The current evidence says authentication is now required for the modern live-timing endpoint, and FastF1 supports Access authentication, but channel availability still depends on what F1 actually publishes during a session. citeturn420353search6turn216203search1

So tomorrow's test should explicitly answer:

**Authenticated Official SignalR → non-zero Position.z + CarData.z?**

If yes, that could materially change RaceVision's live-motion provider hierarchy. If no, the Baku dual-feed architecture remains appropriate.

And because your Baku test showed that OpenF1 already gives the motion data while Official gives the authoritative timing, we should **capture both during FP3 and Qualifying rather than switching immediately to one source**. fileciteturn0file0L20-L22

## Assistant

Yes — I’ve reviewed and understood the **entire P3 report**, and this materially changes the RaceVision live-data decision.

### My assessment

The most important result is this:

> **F1 TV authentication works, and Official F1 is now delivering live `Position.z` and `CarData.z`.**

P3 received **4,559 `Position.z` frames** and **4,551 `CarData.z` frames**, whereas Thursday FP1/FP2 had zero for both. That is the clearest possible validation that the JWT was the missing authentication boundary. fileciteturn1file0L13-L16

Even more important, Official F1 was **closer to live**:

- Official `Position.z`: ~**2.1–2.3 s p50**
- OpenF1 location: ~**4.6–4.7 s p50**
- OpenF1 suffered an observed ~**15 s end-of-session backlog**
- Official stayed around ~**2.1 s** at the end. fileciteturn1file0L128-L142

That means the architecture I would now use is:

```text
                    ┌─────────────────────────┐
                    │   Official F1 SignalR   │
                    │      F1 TV JWT           │
                    └────────────┬────────────┘
                                 │
                    TimingData / Position.z
                         / CarData.z
                                 │
                                 ▼
                     ┌─────────────────────┐
                     │ RaceVision Provider │
                     │ / Canonical Runtime │
                     └──────────┬──────────┘
                                │
                 ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐
                 │              │              │
                 ▼              ▼              ▼
             Unity 3D       Prediction      Analytics
                 │
                 ▼
              Director
                 │
                 ▼
              Streaming


        ┌─────────────────────────┐
        │      OpenF1 MQTT        │
        │ location / car_data     │
        └────────────┬────────────┘
                     │
                     ▼
              FALLBACK / A-B
```

### I would now change one thing from the earlier Baku decision

Before P3, we had:

**Official = timing**  
**OpenF1 = live motion**

After P3, I would formally change that to:

**Official JWT = primary timing + primary live pose/car telemetry**  
**OpenF1 = independent fallback + validation/reference feed**

That is exactly what the P3 evidence supports. fileciteturn1file0L20-L27

And I would **keep OpenF1**, rather than removing it.

That gives RaceVision something extremely valuable: **live redundancy**.

If the F1 TV authentication expires, the Official WebSocket fails, or F1 changes a feed, RaceVision can automatically fall back to OpenF1 instead of losing the cars.

---

## The really important architectural discovery

The Official `.z` stream is **batched**.

The report shows approximately:

> ~1.0 Position.z field update/sec

but those frames contain **multiple cars**, rather than one row per car. fileciteturn1file0L117-L126

That means **do not compare Official frame count directly with OpenF1 message count**.

OpenF1:

```text
~6.5 Hz/car
```

Official:

```text
~1 batched snapshot/sec
```

Those are different transport representations.

What matters to RaceVision is the **effective per-car sample cadence and timestamp spacing after decoding**, not raw MQTT/WebSocket message count.

This is important when we configure the interpolation/prediction system.

---

# I would NOT simply turn on the existing interpolation and call the job finished

The report recommends 150–200 ms interpolation, which is sensible. fileciteturn1file0L219-L225

But now that we have real Official `.z`, I think RaceVision should use this opportunity to make the motion pipeline much more deliberate:

```text
Official Position.z
       ↓
decode
       ↓
timestamp normalization
       ↓
per-car state
       ↓
gap detection
       ↓
150–200 ms presentation buffer
       ↓
cubic interpolation
       ↓
heading / orientation estimation
       ↓
PredictionSmoother
       ↓
60/120 Hz Unity presentation
```

The key point is that **Phase 15 prediction should remain downstream of telemetry ingestion**, rather than trying to predict raw OpenF1 packets.

That keeps the architecture clean.

---

# The biggest thing I would change in RaceVision now

I would add **provenance to the canonical telemetry state**.

For example:

```text
source = OFFICIAL_F1
source_channel = POSITION_Z
authenticated = true
received_at = ...
source_timestamp = ...
sequence/version = ...
quality = ...
```

and for fallback:

```text
source = OPENF1
source_channel = LOCATION
authenticated = true
...
```

Why?

Because RaceVision will eventually have situations such as:

```text
Official available
OpenF1 available
Official delayed
OpenF1 delayed
Official temporarily missing
OpenF1 temporarily missing
```

The renderer shouldn't need to know any of that.

The provider/telemetry layer should decide:

> “Here is the best canonical car state currently available.”

Unity should simply consume canonical state.

That fits the architecture you've already built.

---

# I also strongly recommend automatic source failover

Something along these lines:

```text
                Official JWT
                    │
              healthy?
               /       \
             YES        NO
              │          │
              ▼          ▼
       Official pose   OpenF1 pose
              │          │
              └────┬─────┘
                   ▼
            Canonical state
```

But don't switch sources every time one packet is late.

Use a health state such as:

```text
HEALTHY
DEGRADED
FAILED
RECOVERING
```

with hysteresis.

That prevents:

```text
Official
OpenF1
Official
OpenF1
Official
```

every time there's a small timing difference.

---

# The JWT issue is now the most important operational concern

Your report says:

> `SubscriptionStatus=active`

and:

> **F1 TV Access Monthly**

with expiry **28 September 2026**. fileciteturn1file0L44-L49

The report also explicitly recommends rotating the JWT after it was pasted into chat. fileciteturn1file0L219-L226

I agree with that recommendation.

**Do not put the JWT in the RaceVision repository, GitHub, Cursor prompt, logs, screenshots, or frontend.**

Keep the credential in the local authentication mechanism/file, and have the Official provider retrieve it securely.

And because the subscription/token is short-lived, **token expiry/refresh must become a real provider concern**, not a manual race-weekend procedure.

---

# One particularly useful discovery

The report found this working connection pattern:

```text
negotiate
   ↓
WebSocket
   ?authToken=<token>
```

and that adding `id=` to the Python WebSocket URL resulted in **404**. fileciteturn1file0L44-L47

That is exactly the sort of implementation detail that should now be incorporated into the **production `OfficialF1SignalRProvider`**, rather than leaving it in the experimental `logs/baku_weekend_web` implementation.

This is a strong candidate for the next RaceVision engineering task.

---

# I would also keep the dual capture for Qualifying

The P3 evidence is excellent, but I would **not declare the experiment completely finished yet**.

Qualifying is valuable because it gives us a much more demanding test of the complete system:

**Official JWT + OpenF1 + Timing + `.z` + interpolation + failover + persistence**

And Qualifying should reveal behaviour under much more intense timing/telemetry activity.

The report itself recommends the next step as:

> **Qualifying — same dual capture + compare.** fileciteturn1file0L219-L227

I agree.

---

# There is also an important storage conclusion

The P3 capture was approximately:

**860 MB for one practice capture**, including:

- Official JSONL: 51.3 MB
- OpenF1 JSONL: 524.7 MB
- receive log: 283.5 MB. fileciteturn1file0L192-L203

So the production system absolutely should **not** become a “save every packet forever” system.

I would make the production architecture:

```text
LIVE RAW FEEDS
      │
      ├── short-lived diagnostic/raw buffer
      │
      ▼
Canonical telemetry
      │
      ├── RAM latest state
      ├── ~10 Hz persisted samples
      ├── session events
      └── derived analytics
```

That is much more compatible with the PostgreSQL/database architecture you've already built.

---

# What I now consider the RaceVision provider hierarchy

### Primary

**Official F1 + F1 TV JWT**

For:

- TimingData
- Position.z
- CarData.z
- Session state
- TrackStatus
- DriverList
- RaceControl
- weather
- authoritative timing/classification

### Secondary

**OpenF1 MQTT**

For:

- fallback live position
- fallback car data
- independent cross-checking
- resilience if Official JWT/authentication/feed fails
- experimental comparison

### Historical / archive

**Official F1 static archive + FastF1/other historical providers**

This is particularly valuable because your Baku P2 archive reached `Complete`, allowing offline `.z` analysis. The P3 archive was still generating at close. fileciteturn1file0L9-L15

---

# Bottom line

**P3 has validated the missing piece of RaceVision's live architecture.**

Thursday established that OpenF1 could provide the motion.

**Friday established that authenticated Official F1 can provide the motion too — and with lower measured latency.**

So I would now regard the architecture as:

> **Official F1 authenticated telemetry is the primary live source; OpenF1 remains the resilient secondary source.**

And I would **not** redesign Phases 10–15 around this discovery. Instead, integrate it through the existing provider/canonical telemetry boundaries, then let the existing synchronization, prediction, Unity and director layers consume it.

The next engineering milestone I would focus on is **porting the proven JWT `.z` connection from the Baku prototype into `OfficialF1SignalRProvider`, adding secure token lifecycle + source failover, and then validating the complete path during Qualifying**.

## Assistant

Yes — I’ve inspected the **actual P3 Official JSONL file you uploaded**, not just the report, and that lets me answer this much more precisely.

## The answer

**It gives you a very strong F1 live telemetry dataset for RaceVision, but I would not call it “full telemetry” in the engineering/ECU sense.**

For RaceVision's intended 3D digital twin, however, it contains the **core live car telemetry you need**.

### What your actual P3 file contains

The captured Official F1 feed contains:

| Area | What is actually present |
|---|---|
| **Position** | X, Y, Z for cars + `Status` |
| **Car telemetry** | RPM, speed, gear, throttle, brake |
| **Timing** | Lap/sector information, positions, gaps, best laps, speeds, pit state |
| **Tyres** | Current tyre compound and stint information |
| **Session** | Session status, session info, clock |
| **Track** | Track-status/flags, including VSC |
| **Race control** | Official race-control messages |
| **Weather** | Weather data and series |
| **Drivers** | Driver/team identity |
| **Team radio metadata** | Radio capture notifications/path |
| **Pit lane** | Pit-lane timing |
| **Lap tracking** | Lap series / driver tracker |

The raw `CarData.z` in your file contains **five channels**, and the observed values map to the standard telemetry set of RPM, speed, gear, throttle and brake. FastF1 documents the corresponding standard car telemetry channels as Speed, RPM, nGear, Throttle, Brake and DRS; the Position data are X/Y/Z plus Status. citeturn166944search0

Your P3 capture also contains **non-zero X/Y/Z position records**, so this is not merely a timing feed. It is a genuine live car-motion dataset.

### What it does NOT give you

It is **not the same thing as the complete internal telemetry available to an F1 team**.

Your file does not contain the kind of detailed engineering channels you would expect from a team's car sensors, such as:

- individual wheel speeds
- steering angle
- suspension travel
- suspension damper data
- tyre pressure
- individual tyre carcass/surface temperatures
- brake temperatures/pressure
- detailed fuel quantity/flow
- detailed ERS battery state/energy flows
- engine temperatures/pressures
- aerodynamic loads
- ride height
- accelerometer channels
- torque/load channels
- detailed power-unit operating modes
- gearbox/clutch engineering channels

Those are **not present in the captured Official feed** you supplied.

There are also Official topics that remained silent during P3, including `DriverRaceInfo`, `DriverScore`, `OvertakeSeries`, `PitStopSeries`, and others. fileciteturn1file0L55-L83

## But there is an important distinction for RaceVision

For the **RaceVision digital twin**, your P3 data is much closer to:

> **“full broadcast/live timing telemetry”**

than to:

> **“full F1 engineering telemetry.”**

That is a very important difference.

Your actual file gives RaceVision enough to build:

```text
Car
 ├── X
 ├── Y
 ├── Z
 ├── Speed
 ├── RPM
 ├── Gear
 ├── Throttle
 ├── Brake
 ├── Lap
 ├── Sector
 ├── Position
 ├── Gap
 ├── Tyre compound
 ├── Stint
 ├── Pit state
 ├── Track status
 └── session/race-control context
```

That is already enough to drive a **very convincing live 3D F1 simulation/broadcast twin**.

FastF1's documented telemetry model also confirms that the standard accessible telemetry includes Speed, RPM, gear, throttle and brake, alongside X/Y/Z position. citeturn166944search0

## One thing I noticed in your actual file

The `CarData.z` values show the data-quality issue we already identified:

**Throttle/brake can contain `104`**, which is a sentinel/unavailable value rather than genuine pedal percentage. FastF1 also documents `104` as an error/unavailable value for throttle data. citeturn166944search0

So RaceVision should absolutely normalize that before analytics:

```text
104 → null / unavailable
```

rather than:

```text
104% throttle
104% brake
```

That will matter enormously once Phase 16 analytics starts calculating driver performance.

## The exciting part for RaceVision

Your P3 file proves that you now have all of the following **from the same authenticated Official F1 session**:

```text
           OFFICIAL F1 + JWT
                  │
        ┌─────────┴─────────┐
        │                   │
   Position.z          CarData.z
        │                   │
      X/Y/Z        RPM / Speed / Gear
        │             Throttle / Brake
        └─────────┬─────────┘
                  │
             TimingData
                  │
       Lap / Sector / Gap
                  │
       Tyres / Flags / Weather
                  │
                  ▼
          RACEVISION CANONICAL
                  │
        ┌─────────┼─────────┐
        ▼         ▼         ▼
      Unity   Prediction  Analytics
        │
        ▼
     Director
        │
        ▼
   Broadcast / Stream
```

That is **far more complete for your purpose** than the situation we had Thursday.

Thursday was essentially:

**Official timing + OpenF1 motion**

P3 is now:

**Official authenticated timing + Official authenticated motion + Official car telemetry**

And your measurements show Official Position.z was around **2.1–2.3 seconds from source timestamp to receive**, versus roughly **4.6–4.7 seconds for OpenF1 location** during the middle of P3. fileciteturn1file0L128-L142

## My recommendation

I would now classify the Official JWT feed as:

**RaceVision Primary Live Telemetry Source**

and OpenF1 as:

**Secondary/Fallback/Validation Source**

rather than treating OpenF1 as the primary motion provider.

The P3 report already reaches that same architecture: Official `Position.z` as primary pose, Official `CarData.z` for live car telemetry, Official `TimingData` for timing, and OpenF1 as fallback. fileciteturn1file0L219-L227

### One caveat

I would **not yet claim that this is “100% complete RaceVision telemetry”** until we test the same channels during **Qualifying and then the Race**. Practice showed the authenticated channels work, but some feeds are session-dependent and some topics are silent depending on session type. fileciteturn1file0L53-L83

So the best description is:

> **P3 has proven that F1 TV JWT gives RaceVision the core official live motion + car telemetry required for the digital twin, but it is not complete team-level engineering telemetry.**

And importantly, **the uploaded P3 JSONL is real evidence of that**, rather than an assumption from the documentation.

## 1. What I would make the objective of Qualifying

Do **not** make the objective simply:

> “Did Position.z arrive?”

P3 already proved that.

The Qualifying objective should be:

> **Can RaceVision reliably construct one canonical, low-latency, production-quality car state from Official F1 JWT telemetry, with OpenF1 available as an independent fallback, while preserving authoritative timing and surviving Q1/Q2/Q3 transitions?**

That is the next real architectural test.

Your P3 result already established the essential ingredients: Official `Position.z` and `CarData.z` became live with JWT, Official pose was around 2.1–2.3 s p50, and OpenF1 was slower and could backlog near session end. fileciteturn1file0L20-L27 fileciteturn1file0L128-L142

---

# 2. The architecture I would test

For Qualifying, run both feeds simultaneously:

```text
                   BAKU QUALIFYING
                         │
          ┌──────────────┴──────────────┐
          │                             │
          ▼                             ▼
   Official F1 SignalR             OpenF1 MQTT
      + F1 TV JWT                   authenticated
          │                             │
          │                             │
   ┌──────┼────────┐             ┌──────┼─────────┐
   │      │        │             │      │         │
Position CarData Timing      location car_data laps
   .z     .z     Data
   │      │        │
   └──────┼────────┘
          │
          ▼
   Provider / Join Layer
          │
          ▼
    Canonical Car State
          │
    ┌─────┼───────┐
    ▼     ▼       ▼
 Unity  Prediction Analytics
    │
    ▼
 Director / Broadcast
```

### Official should supply

**Primary:**

`Position.z`  
`CarData.z`  
`TimingData` / `TimingDataF1`

and:

`DriverList`  
`SessionInfo`  
`SessionStatus`  
`TrackStatus`  
`RaceControlMessages`  
`WeatherData`  
`TyreStintSeries`  
`CurrentTyres`  
`TimingStats`  
`LapSeries`  
`DriverTracker`  
`PitLaneTimeCollection`  
`TopThree`  
`TeamRadio`  
`ExtrapolatedClock`

P3 demonstrated all of these types of feeds, with 24 Official topics actually producing data. fileciteturn1file0L55-L83

### OpenF1 should supply

Keep:

`location`  
`car_data`  
`laps`

and continue capturing:

`position`  
`stints`  
`race_control`  
`pit`  
`weather`  
`team_radio`

OpenF1's current documentation recommends MQTT for backend real-time ingestion, which matches the RaceVision architecture. citeturn134073search0

---

# 3. Very important: do not change RaceVision production code before Qualifying

This is the biggest recommendation I would make.

You now have a **working experimental implementation** in `logs/baku_weekend_web`, and P3 has already proven the JWT connection.

I would **not rush the production provider integration immediately before Qualifying**.

Instead:

```text
Qualifying
    ↓
capture evidence
    ↓
analyse
    ↓
lock architecture
    ↓
port into RaceVision
    ↓
integration tests
    ↓
Race validation
```

That gives you an extremely valuable clean baseline.

You don't want a production-code change during the first high-intensity test to make it unclear whether a later problem came from:

**F1 → network → JWT → prototype → RaceVision integration**

or from the new code itself.

---

# 4. Pre-Qualifying checklist

Start the capture approximately **30–45 minutes before 13:00 BST**.

## A. Official authentication

Verify:

```text
F1 TV authenticated = YES
SubscriptionStatus = active
Token available = YES
Token not expired during session
```

Your P3 report recorded an active **F1 TV Access Monthly** subscription token with an observed expiry of **28 September 2026**, which should cover Qualifying and the Saturday race, subject of course to the token remaining valid. fileciteturn1file0L44-L49

Do not print the JWT.

Do not put it in the JSONL.

Do not put it in Git.

Do not send it to the frontend.

---

# 5. Capture should begin before the session begins

This matters because we want to understand the transition:

```text
pre-session
     ↓
pit exit opens
     ↓
first car moves
     ↓
Q1
     ↓
Q2
     ↓
Q3
     ↓
chequered flag
     ↓
session finalised
     ↓
archive complete
```

Your P3 capture actually illustrates why this is useful: the file contains pre-session Position/CarData entries before the cars are producing meaningful motion, then the session transitions into live activity. We should preserve those transitions for the provider state machine.

---

# 6. Record a proper run manifest

Every Qualifying capture should have something like:

```text
run_id
meeting_key
session_key
session_name
start_time
end_time
official_auth_status
openf1_auth_status
official_topics_requested
openf1_topics_requested
machine_clock_utc
software/git revision
```

For example:

```text
meeting_key = 1295
session = Qualifying
```

The session key must be discovered dynamically.

**Do not hard-code the P3 key `11372`.**

---

# 7. Measure both feeds separately

This is critical.

Do not just count messages.

For every stream measure:

### Connection health

```text
connected
disconnected
reconnected
disconnect duration
authentication failures
subscription failures
```

### Latency

For every usable packet:

```text
source_timestamp
received_timestamp
delay_ms
```

Then calculate:

```text
p50
p75
p95
p99
maximum
```

P3 established the baseline:

| Feed | P3 measured latency |
|---|---:|
| Official Position.z | ~2.1–2.3 s p50 |
| OpenF1 location | ~4.6–4.7 s p50 |
| OpenF1 location end drain | ~15 s |

fileciteturn1file0L128-L142

For Qualifying, we want to know whether that behaviour remains stable when the session gets much more intense.

---

# 8. Measure Position.z properly

Do **not** use:

> `4,559 frames`

as the main quality metric.

P3 taught us that Official Position.z is **batched**, so one frame can contain many cars. fileciteturn1file0L117-L126

Instead calculate per driver:

```text
driver_number
valid samples
non-zero samples
first valid timestamp
last valid timestamp
median sample interval
p95 sample interval
maximum gap
number of status changes
```

Do this independently for all 22 cars.

The question becomes:

> When car #63 is actually active, how consistently are we receiving its position?

That is what the Unity runtime cares about.

---

# 9. Do the same for CarData.z

For every driver:

```text
RPM
Speed
Gear
Throttle
Brake
```

Record:

```text
sample count
valid count
invalid count
zero count
sentinel count
min
max
median
```

Most importantly, explicitly count the sentinel values.

Your P3 report already identified the known problem with `104` for throttle/brake. fileciteturn1file0L209-L215

RaceVision should transform this during canonicalisation:

```text
104 → unavailable/null
```

rather than allowing it into analytics as:

```text
104%
```

---

# 10. Test whether Position and CarData actually line up

This is one of the most important new tests.

For every car:

```text
Position.z timestamp
        ↓
nearest CarData.z timestamp
        ↓
compare state
```

We want to see whether:

```text
Position timestamp
≈
CarData timestamp
```

and whether the values represent the same moment.

Then check:

```text
position movement
        ↕
CarData speed
```

We don't need them to be mathematically identical because coordinate processing and sample timing differ.

We **do** want strong temporal consistency.

This is the first step toward a genuine:

**canonical vehicle state**

rather than two unrelated streams.

---

# 11. Test the Official → OpenF1 join

This deserves its own dataset.

For each driver create:

```text
driver_number
official_position_timestamp
official_x
official_y
official_z

openf1_timestamp
openf1_x
openf1_y
openf1_z

time_delta
spatial_delta
source_used
```

Do **not** match using:

```text
wall-clock receive time
```

because the feeds have different network delays.

Instead compare their **source timestamps**, then calculate the receive-time delay separately.

This distinction is crucial.

---

# 12. Test the Q1 → Q2 → Q3 transitions

This is probably the biggest additional value of Qualifying.

We need to prove that RaceVision doesn't assume:

```text
Practice
```

behaviour.

Instead explicitly record:

```text
SESSION START
Q1 START
Q1 END
Q2 START
Q2 END
Q3 START
Q3 END
SESSION FINAL
```

And associate every telemetry packet with the correct session phase.

For example:

```text
session_key = 11373
phase = Q1
```

then:

```text
session_key = 11373
phase = Q2
```

The **session key remains the same**, while the qualifying phase changes.

That distinction needs to be represented in RaceVision's canonical/session state.

---

# 13. Test pit entry and exit

Qualifying is ideal for this.

For each car record:

```text
on track
pit lane
pit entry
pit exit
garage/not running
```

and compare:

**Official status**

against

**position state**

against

**timing state**

against

**OpenF1 state**

This will help the Unity runtime avoid doing silly things like extrapolating a car around the circuit while it is actually sitting in the garage.

---

# 14. Test track status and interruptions

Capture:

```text
GREEN
YELLOW
DOUBLE YELLOW
VSC
RED FLAG
SAFETY-related state if provided
CHEQUERED
ALL CLEAR
```

You don't want the prediction engine to behave the same way during:

```text
full-speed running
```

and:

```text
VSC / red flag
```

That feeds directly into your Phase 15 prediction/failure architecture.

---

# 15. Keep OpenF1 running even when Official looks perfect

This is important.

Do **not** turn OpenF1 off simply because Official JWT works.

OpenF1 now has a different purpose:

### It becomes your independent reference/fallback feed.

OpenF1 authentication currently supports real-time MQTT and WebSockets and explicitly recommends MQTT for backend real-time ingestion. citeturn134073search0

So during Qualifying, capture both:

```text
Official
+
OpenF1
```

for the entire session.

---

# 16. Test failover safely

I would **not intentionally unplug the Official F1 feed during Q1/Q2/Q3**.

Instead do this:

```text
Official capture ────────────────┐
                                  ▼
                             captured data
                                  │
OpenF1 capture ───────────────────┘
```

Then after the session:

```text
disable Official
       ↓
run the source arbitration
       ↓
verify OpenF1 becomes active
```

That tests the exact failover logic without risking the actual qualifying capture.

Eventually RaceVision should behave like:

```text
Official healthy
      ↓
USE OFFICIAL


Official degraded
      ↓
monitor


Official failed
      ↓
USE OPENF1


Official recovers
      ↓
stabilise


Official healthy
      ↓
RETURN TO OFFICIAL
```

Do not switch providers on one missing packet.

Use a health/degraded/failed/recovery state machine.

---

# 17. Test interpolation, but don't confuse interpolation with more telemetry

This is especially important.

Your Official feed can arrive as approximately one batched Position.z update per second while still carrying multiple cars. fileciteturn1file0L117-L126

Therefore:

```text
60 FPS Unity
```

does **not** mean:

```text
60 Hz telemetry
```

RaceVision should create smooth presentation from the available samples:

```text
Telemetry samples
       ↓
timestamped state history
       ↓
150–200 ms presentation buffer
       ↓
cubic interpolation
       ↓
heading estimation
       ↓
PredictionSmoother
       ↓
60/120 FPS rendering
```

That is exactly where your Phase 14/15 work becomes valuable.

---

# 18. Qualifying is the first opportunity to test a true canonical state

I would define the canonical vehicle state roughly as:

```text
VehicleState
│
├── identity
│   ├── racing_number
│   ├── driver
│   └── team
│
├── position
│   ├── x
│   ├── y
│   ├── z
│   └── status
│
├── motion
│   ├── speed
│   ├── rpm
│   ├── gear
│   ├── throttle
│   └── brake
│
├── race state
│   ├── position
│   ├── lap
│   ├── sector
│   ├── gap
│   └── pit state
│
├── tyres
│   ├── compound
│   └── stint
│
├── session
│   ├── session_key
│   └── quali_phase
│
└── provenance
    ├── source
    ├── source_timestamp
    ├── received_timestamp
    ├── latency
    └── quality
```

That would be the object Unity, prediction and analytics eventually consume.

They should **not** consume `Position.z` or `v1/location` directly.

---

# 19. Measure data quality separately from data quantity

I would produce a Qualifying table like:

| Metric | Official JWT | OpenF1 |
|---|---:|---:|
| Position samples | | |
| CarData samples | | |
| Active cars | | |
| Median latency | | |
| P95 latency | | |
| Maximum latency | | |
| Reconnects | | |
| Missing intervals | | |
| Invalid values | | |
| `104` pedal values | | |
| Non-zero XYZ | | |
| Per-car coverage | | |
| End-session backlog | | |

This will give us much more useful evidence than simply saying:

> “The WebSocket stayed connected.”

---

# 20. Measure the storage problem again

Your P3 run was already roughly:

**860 MB**

with:

- Official JSONL ~51.3 MB
- OpenF1 JSONL ~524.7 MB
- receive log ~283.5 MB. fileciteturn1file0L192-L203

Qualifying is likely to be another excellent stress test.

But **do not use this as the production database design**.

For RaceVision production:

```text
Raw packet
   ↓
short debug buffer
   ↓
decoder
   ↓
canonical state
   ↓
~10 Hz persistence
   ↓
derived analytics
```

Not:

```text
every MQTT/WebSocket packet
        ↓
PostgreSQL forever
```

---

# 21. What I would persist in PostgreSQL

For the real RaceVision store I would concentrate on:

### Vehicle telemetry sample

```text
session_id
timestamp
driver_id
x
y
z
speed
rpm
gear
throttle
brake
status
source
quality
```

### Timing

```text
session_id
timestamp
driver_id
lap
sector
position
gap
best_lap
last_lap
```

### Race/session events

```text
timestamp
session_id
event_type
event_payload
source
```

### Tyres

```text
driver_id
compound
stint
new_used
lap_start
lap_end
```

That is enough for the majority of your future analytics without carrying gigabytes of raw transport messages into PostgreSQL.

---

# 22. One thing I want Qualifying to test specifically for Phase 15

Phase 15 already has prediction/correction infrastructure.

Qualifying should give us real measurements for:

```text
raw Official timestamp
        ↓
network arrival
        ↓
canonical state
        ↓
prediction
        ↓
presentation
```

Measure:

```text
source → receive
receive → canonical
canonical → presentation
```

Then:

```text
prediction error
```

when the next real sample arrives.

This gives RaceVision a real **prediction accuracy dataset**, instead of testing prediction mostly with synthetic or historical inputs.

That could become extremely valuable for Phase 15's next refinement.

---

# 23. What to do with the Official archive after Qualifying

Do **not** stop when the chequered flag falls.

Wait for:

```text
SessionStatus = Finalised
```

then monitor:

```text
ArchiveStatus
```

until:

```text
Complete
```

P2 demonstrated why this matters: the archive eventually became `Complete`, opening up the possibility of offline `.z` validation. fileciteturn0file0L68-L72

Then perform:

```text
LIVE Position.z
        ↕
ARCHIVED Position.z

LIVE CarData.z
        ↕
ARCHIVED CarData.z
```

That is an excellent validation of the decoder and storage pipeline.

---

# 24. The most valuable comparison after Qualifying

I would generate this exact comparison:

### Official JWT

```text
Position.z
CarData.z
TimingData
```

versus

### OpenF1

```text
location
car_data
laps
```

and calculate:

```text
latency
sample density
per-car coverage
position agreement
speed agreement
session continuity
reconnects
end-session backlog
```

Then we can formally settle the production provider contract.

---

# 25. Acceptance gates I would use

I would **not** use a simplistic:

> “all tests passed”

criterion.

I'd want the Qualifying evidence to show:

### Gate A — Authentication

```text
Official JWT authentication successful
```

### Gate B — Motion

```text
Position.z available
CarData.z available
```

for active cars.

### Gate C — Timing

```text
Official TimingData continuous
```

### Gate D — Join

```text
Position + CarData + Timing
can be associated by driver and source timestamp
```

### Gate E — Qualifying phases

```text
Q1/Q2/Q3 correctly identified
```

### Gate F — Latency

Official remains measurably ahead of OpenF1 under comparable conditions, without assuming P3's exact numbers must repeat. P3 provides the baseline of roughly 2.1–2.3 s for Official Position.z and 4.6–4.7 s for OpenF1 location. fileciteturn1file0L128-L142

### Gate G — Fallback

```text
OpenF1 can provide motion if Official becomes unavailable
```

### Gate H — Data quality

```text
104 throttle/brake values sanitised
invalid/null channels identified
```

### Gate I — Storage

```text
canonical state can be persisted without raw-transport explosion
```

### Gate J — Archive

```text
post-session Official archive captured when Complete
```

# 28. One extra experiment I'd add

Because the ultimate objective is **RaceVision**, not merely telemetry collection, I would run one additional metric:

### Position → rendered-motion quality

For perhaps 5–10 representative cars:

```text
raw Official Position.z
        ↓
interpolated trajectory
        ↓
predicted trajectory
        ↓
actual next sample
```

Measure:

```text

heading error
```

That will tell us whether the existing **150–200 ms interpolation + Phase 15 prediction** is actually good enough for the Unity digital twin.

# 29. My expected architectural outcome

Assuming Qualifying behaves like P3, I would then lock this into RaceVision:

```text
OFFICIAL F1 + JWT
       │
       ├── TimingData
       ├── Position.z
       ├── CarData.z
       ├── Session
       ├── TrackStatus
       ├── Drivers
       ├── Tyres
       ├── Weather
       └── Race Control
              │
              ▼
       OfficialF1SignalRProvider
              │
              ▼
       Canonical Telemetry
              │
       ┌──────┼─────────┐
       ▼      ▼         ▼
   Sync   Prediction  Analytics
       │
       ▼
      Unity
       │
       ▼
    Director


OPENF1 MQTT
    │
    ├── location
    ├── car_data
    └── laps
    │
    ▼
Fallback / Validation Provider
```

That is much cleaner than the original Thursday architecture.

---

# 30. Then the next RaceVision implementation

**After Qualifying**, I would have Cursor implement approximately this sequence:

**A. Official JWT lifecycle**  
Secure token loading, validation, refresh/expiry state.

**B. Production Official `.z` ingestion**  
Move the proven connection logic into `OfficialF1SignalRProvider`.

**C. Canonical provider join**  
Official Position + CarData + Timing.

**D. Source health manager**  
Official primary / OpenF1 fallback.

**E. Telemetry provenance**  
Every canonical sample knows where it came from.

**F. Production interpolation**  
150–200 ms presentation buffer, using the existing synchronization/prediction architecture.

**G. 10 Hz persistence**  
Canonical samples rather than raw transport packets.

**H. Qualifying integration tests**  
Use today's captured data as real fixtures.

**I. Archive replay test**  
Compare live `.z` with archived `.z`.

That would turn today's Qualifying from simply another data capture into a **formal RaceVision live-telemetry qualification test**.

### The single most important point

**I would not use Qualifying to “build more stuff.” I would use it to collect the evidence that tells us exactly what should be built.**

P3 already proved:

> **JWT unlocks Official live pose and car telemetry.**

Qualifying should now prove:

> **Official JWT + canonical join + interpolation + prediction + OpenF1 fallback can form a reliable RaceVision live vehicle state during a complex multi-phase session.**

That is the evidence I would want before making this the permanent foundation of RaceVision.

fileciteturn1file0L20-L27

## Assistant

Yes — I’ve taken this additional **P3 data-examples report** into account. It adds an important level of detail beyond the earlier summary because it shows the **actual field-level relationship** between Official F1 JWT and OpenF1.

My input is that this makes the RaceVision architecture even clearer.

### 1. The Official feed is more capable than we originally thought

The important discovery is not merely that `Position.z` and `CarData.z` exist.

We now have a concrete field mapping:

```text
Official CarData.z
0  = RPM
2  = Speed
3  = Gear
4  = Throttle
5  = Brake
45 = DRS
```

and:

```text
Official Position.z
X
Y
Z
Status
```

That is a very strong foundation for your Unity vehicle runtime.

So for RaceVision, the core live vehicle object can now be constructed from the **Official F1 feed alone**, without needing OpenF1 for the basic car-motion/engine-state path.

---

# 2. OpenF1 is now more valuable as a verification system

The sample for car #3 is very useful:

Official:

```text
X -2915
Y -2176
Z -233
```

OpenF1:

```text
X -3321
Y -2348
Z -232
```

These are not supposed to be identical because the source timestamps differ:

```text
Official 08:59:58.766
OpenF1    08:59:58.226
```

and their receive times are also different.

That means we should **never join the feeds using arrival time**.

RaceVision should join them using:

```text
driver number
+
source timestamp
```

with an explicit temporal tolerance.

That is an important design rule.

---

# 3. I would add a “source timestamp” to every canonical sample

This is now even more important.

A canonical telemetry object should contain at least:

```text
source
source_timestamp
received_timestamp
driver_number
```

and perhaps:

```text
latency_ms
```

Then:

```text
Official Position.z
       │
       ├── source timestamp
       └── received timestamp

OpenF1 location
       │
       ├── source timestamp
       └── received timestamp
```

can be compared correctly.

This will also be very useful for your Phase 15 prediction engine.

---

# 4. There is a major reason NOT to blindly merge OpenF1 and Official values

The weather example demonstrates this.

Official:

```text
Wind direction 326
Wind speed 2.3
```

OpenF1:

```text
Wind direction 230
Wind speed 4.5
```

Yet the other weather values are quite close.

That tells us something important:

**Two feeds are not necessarily interchangeable even when they describe the same physical phenomenon.**

Therefore RaceVision needs a **source authority policy per field**, not just:

> “Official is primary, OpenF1 is secondary.”

For example:

```text
Timing classification
        → Official

Position XYZ
        → Official JWT

RPM / Speed / Gear / Throttle / Brake / DRS
        → Official JWT

Fallback position
        → OpenF1

Fallback car telemetry
        → OpenF1

Completed-lap analytics
        → OpenF1 can be convenient

Published weather
        → Official
```

This is much safer than blindly averaging or merging two feeds.

---

# 5. The timing discrepancy is also important

Your example shows:

Official:

```text
BestLapTime = 1:44.126
```

OpenF1:

```text
best = 1:43.922
```

That reinforces the rule you've already established:

**Official TimingData should be the authoritative published timing/classification source.**

OpenF1 can still be extremely useful for analytics because its completed-lap data is easy to work with, but RaceVision should not silently replace an Official timing result with an OpenF1 result.

---

# 6. This also proves why a provider-agnostic canonical model is necessary

I would now strongly recommend that RaceVision does **not** expose provider-specific objects beyond the provider boundary.

Bad:

```text
Unity
  ↓
Position.z
CarData.z
OpenF1.location
```

Better:

```text
Official Provider ──┐
                    ├──> CanonicalVehicleState
OpenF1 Provider ────┘
                              │
                              ├── Unity
                              ├── Prediction
                              ├── Analytics
                              └── Director
```

Then RaceVision doesn't care whether a position came from:

```text
Official Position.z
```

or:

```text
OpenF1 location
```

The provider arbitration layer handles that.

---

# 7. I would add field-level provenance

This is something I would now consider particularly important.

Instead of only:

```text
source = OFFICIAL
```

I'd allow:

```text
position_source = OFFICIAL
car_data_source = OFFICIAL
timing_source = OFFICIAL
weather_source = OFFICIAL
```

That becomes powerful during failover.

Suppose:

```text
Official Position.z      OK
Official CarData.z      OK
Official TimingData     OK
```

then:

```text
position_source = OFFICIAL
car_data_source = OFFICIAL
timing_source = OFFICIAL
```

If Official pose disappears:

```text
position_source = OPENF1
car_data_source = OPENF1
timing_source = OFFICIAL
```

RaceVision can continue operating without pretending the whole telemetry system failed.

---

# 8. DRS is a particularly interesting case

Your actual sample establishes:

```text
Official DRS = channel 45 when present
OpenF1 DRS = null
```

So I would **not** use OpenF1 as the authoritative DRS source where the Official stream provides it.

That is exactly the sort of small difference that can become very significant in the Unity visualization.

It means RaceVision can potentially drive:

```text
DRS flap state
```

from Official data rather than trying to infer DRS from speed or another approximation.

---

# 9. The data is good enough to build much more than just cars moving around

With:

```text
X/Y/Z
Speed
RPM
Gear
Throttle
Brake
DRS
Lap
Sector
Position
Gap
Tyre
Stint
Pit state
Track status
Timing
Weather
Race control
```

you have enough information to create useful **derived telemetry**.

For example:

```text
braking zones
acceleration zones
throttle application
gear-selection behaviour
straight-line speed
corner entry speed
corner exit speed
time lost in sectors
lap delta
pit-lane transitions
DRS usage
race-control response
```

And that is where Phase 16 becomes particularly interesting.

You don't need additional proprietary engineering telemetry for RaceVision to perform substantial driver/race analytics.

---

# 10. However, I would NOT call it “full telemetry”

This new report actually makes the boundary clearer.

I'd describe the dataset as:

### **Official F1 public/live telemetry**

rather than:

### **complete F1 car telemetry**

You have the data needed for RaceVision's public-facing digital twin and analytics system, but you do **not** have the team's complete internal sensor/PU/vehicle-engineering dataset.

That distinction should remain explicit in the RaceVision documentation.

---

# 11. One thing I would change in the planned Qualifying test

Originally I suggested measuring message volume and latency.

After seeing these examples, I would add:

### **Cross-feed semantic validation**

For each driver, compare:

```text
POSITION
Official XYZ
       ↕
OpenF1 XYZ

CAR TELEMETRY
Official Speed
       ↕
OpenF1 Speed

Official RPM
       ↕
OpenF1 RPM

Official Gear
       ↕
OpenF1 Gear

Official Throttle
       ↕
OpenF1 Throttle

Official Brake
       ↕
OpenF1 Brake
```

Then calculate:

```text
absolute difference
median difference
p95 difference
maximum difference
timestamp difference
```

That will tell us whether OpenF1 is genuinely a suitable fallback for each field, rather than assuming it is.

For some fields it may be an excellent fallback.

For others, it may only be a reference.

---

# 12. I would also introduce a “field authority matrix”

This could eventually live in RaceVision configuration:

```text
                    PRIMARY             FALLBACK

Position XYZ        Official JWT        OpenF1
Speed               Official JWT        OpenF1
RPM                 Official JWT        OpenF1
Gear                Official JWT        OpenF1
Throttle            Official JWT        OpenF1
Brake               Official JWT        OpenF1
DRS                 Official JWT        OpenF1/unknown

Lap timing          Official            OpenF1
Classification      Official            OpenF1

Tyres               Official            OpenF1
Weather             Official            OpenF1

Race Control        Official            OpenF1
Track Status        Official            OpenF1
Driver identity     Official            REST/OpenF1
```

This is much more robust than a simple provider-level switch.

---

# 13. This gives me one additional architectural recommendation

I would separate:

### **Raw provider telemetry**

from:

### **Canonical telemetry**

from:

### **Derived telemetry**

For example:

```text
RAW
Official Position.z
Official CarData.z
OpenF1 location
OpenF1 car_data

        ↓

CANONICAL
VehicleState

        ↓

DERIVED
velocity vector
heading
acceleration
braking event
cornering state
lap delta
battle state
prediction
confidence
```

Then Phase 16 analytics uses **derived/canonical state**, not the raw Official/OpenF1 formats.

That will prevent the analytics layer becoming dependent on today's particular feed structure.

---

# 14. This is now a very strong foundation for your 3D twin

The combination you've demonstrated gives RaceVision:

```text
                    Official JWT
                         │
              ┌──────────┼───────────┐
              │          │           │
              ▼          ▼           ▼
           Position   CarData     Timing
             XYZ      RPM etc.
              │          │           │
              └────┬─────┴───────────┘
                   │
                   ▼
            Canonical State
                   │
            ┌──────┼───────┐
            ▼      ▼       ▼
         Sync   Prediction Analytics
            │
            ▼
          Unity
            │
            ▼
        Director
            │
            ▼
        Broadcast
```

with OpenF1 sitting beside it as:

```text
       Independent fallback
               +
       independent validation
```

That is a significantly stronger architecture than the one we had before Baku.

---

## My overall conclusion

The new examples **strengthen the case for making Official F1 JWT the primary RaceVision telemetry source**.

More importantly, they tell us *how* to integrate it correctly:

**Don't think “Official feed versus OpenF1 feed.”**

Think:

> **field-by-field authoritative data + independently sourced fallback + timestamp-aware canonicalisation.**

That is the architecture I would take into **Qualifying today**.

And I would make one of today's main objectives:

**prove that Official `Position.z` + Official `CarData.z` + Official `TimingData` can be joined into a stable per-driver canonical state throughout Q1, Q2 and Q3.**

Once that is proven, we have a very strong basis for the next RaceVision implementation step.

Which fields require OpenF1?

Which fields should remain Official-only?

========================================================
PHASE 18 — FAILOVER VALIDATION
========================================================

DO NOT intentionally disrupt the live Official feed during Q1/Q2/Q3.

Instead validate failover logic using captured data after the session.

Model:

OFFICIAL HEALTHY
    -> use Official

OFFICIAL DEGRADED
    -> monitor

OFFICIAL FAILED
    -> OpenF1 fallback

OFFICIAL RECOVERING
    -> stabilisation period

OFFICIAL HEALTHY
    -> return to Official

Test conceptually that RaceVision should avoid rapid source oscillation.

Do not implement production failover yet unless an existing RaceVision failover mechanism already exists.

presentation target
    ->
next real sample

- typical position gap;

- snap events.

150–200 ms

but DO NOT assume this is automatically optimal.

Use Qualifying data to determine whether it remains reasonable.

### Why I would use this exact test

This goes one step beyond P3.

P3 already proved that JWT unlocks the Official motion channels and that Official was closer to live. fileciteturn1file0L20-L27

Your new field-level examples also proved that Official and OpenF1 are **not simply interchangeable representations of the same data**: their timestamps, weather values, timing values and telemetry samples can differ. That is why this test explicitly performs a **timestamp-aware, field-by-field comparison** rather than merely comparing message counts.

The most valuable result today will be establishing whether:

```text
Official Position.z
        +
Official CarData.z
        +
Official TimingData
        ↓
stable per-driver canonical vehicle state
```

works consistently through **Q1 → Q2 → Q3**.

That is the evidence I would use immediately afterward to create the next production RaceVision Cursor agent.

# RaceVision — Baku 2026 Qualifying Telemetry Validation Report

**Official F1 (F1 TV JWT, SignalR) vs OpenF1 (MQTT)** · Meeting 1295 · Session 11373 "Qualifying" · Fri 25 Sep 2026

Capture run `20260925T103903Z` · Report generated Fri 25 Sep 2026 \~20:30 BST

Labels used throughout: **FACT** = measured from the capture/archive; **INFERENCE** = engineering reading of the facts; **RECOMMENDATION** = proposed action.

No token, JWT, SessionId, subscriber id or password appears anywhere in this report.

---

## 1. Executive Summary

**Test status: PASS WITH LIMITATIONS.**

| **QuestionAnswer**                            |                                                                                                                                                                             |
| --------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Official JWT authentication                   | **PASS** — authenticated, subscription active, token valid until 2026-09-28T18:11:45Z                                                                                       |
| Position.z (per-car X/Y/Z)                    | **PASS** — 4,819 frames, 0 decode failures, all 22 cars in every frame, 0 gaps > 2 s in Q1–Q3                                                                               |
| CarData.z (rpm/speed/gear/throttle/brake)     | **PASS** — 4,777 frames, 0 decode failures; DRS channel absent; 104 sentinel + bad gear values need filtering                                                               |
| TimingData                                    | **PASS** — folds to a coherent classification (22 unique positions, no lap regressions)                                                                                     |
| Official latency (Position.z, source→receive) | **p50 2.17–2.23 s, p95 2.64–2.73 s** across Q1/Q2/Q3                                                                                                                        |
| OpenF1 latency (location, source→receive)     | **p50 14.2–14.9 s, p95 24.5–25.6 s** across Q1/Q2/Q3                                                                                                                        |
| Official vs OpenF1 values                     | **Identical.** 179,973 OpenF1 location + 175,566 car_data samples all matched an Official sample at the \*exact same source timestamp\* with **0.00 m / 0 unit difference** |
| Official live vs Official archive             | **Identical.** 18,433 Position and 17,837 CarData samples byte-equal to the published archive                                                                               |
| Continuity                                    | Official: **0** disconnects in Q1–Q3. OpenF1: **24** disconnects (10/7/7) and 13,051 driver-seconds of missing location                                                     |

**Headline (FACT):** OpenF1 `location` and `car_data` are a re-publication of the \*same\* F1 Position.z / CarData.z samples. They arrive \~12 s later than via the JWT and \~22 % of them were lost in Qualifying because the OpenF1 MQTT connection dropped every 90–135 s.

**Headline (RECOMMENDATION):** Official JWT should be treated as the **primary** source for pose, car telemetry, timing, race control and weather in the experimental stack; OpenF1 stays the **fallback** (and the only source when no JWT is available). Production arbitration was **not** changed by this test.

## 10. TimingData Analysis

- Top-level keys: `CutOffPercentage, CutOffTime, Lines, NoEntries, SessionPart, Withheld`.
- TimingData carries **no source timestamp** → its latency cannot be measured directly.

| Q3                                                         | 66,066 | 50,457 | 76.4 % |

## 12. Latency Comparison

Definition: `delay = receive wall-clock − source timestamp` (per sample).

| **StreamQ1 p50 / p95Q2 p50 / p95Q3 p50 / p95Final 2 min Q3 p50** |                    |                    |                    |           |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------ | ------------------ | ------------------ | --------- |
| Official Position.z                                              | 2,233 / 2,732 ms   | 2,187 / 2,665 ms   | 2,171 / 2,637 ms   | 2,161 ms  |
| Official CarData.z                                               | 2,193 / 2,680 ms   | 2,151 / 2,628 ms   | 2,141 / 2,600 ms   | —         |
| OpenF1 location                                                  | 14,888 / 24,508 ms | 14,400 / 25,029 ms | 14,223 / 25,594 ms | 16,746 ms |
| OpenF1 car_data                                                  | 14,649 / —         | 14,142 / —         | 14,130 / —         | —         |

Other: Official RCM p50 1,841 / p95 2,713 ms (second-resolution Utc). OpenF1 race_control in Q3 p50 23,174 ms. Official SessionData \~1.6 s.

Official min 1,525 ms; Official Q max 6,671 ms (Q2). One pre-session outlier (7,791 s) is a cached end-of-P3 frame replayed at feed start — excluded.

**OpenF1 sawtooth (FACT):** delay p50 ≈ 21–26 s in the 20 s before each disconnect, ≈ 4–7 s just after reconnect. Pre-session (low traffic) OpenF1 p50 was 2.7 s.

INFERENCE: OpenF1 delay grows with load until the broker drops the connection. A benchmark after the session showed the hub write path sustains \~1,680 msg/s against \~200 msg/s needed, so our client is **not proven** to be the cause. The Race build now stamps true socket-arrival time and queue wait to settle this (§27).

Position frames are batched (\~4 samples/s per car delivered once per second), so a smooth live renderer needs a presentation delay of p50 2.8 s, p95 3.1 s, p99 3.45 s behind wall clock (≈ 1.2–1.3 s behind the newest sample).

## 13. Q1 Analysis

FACT: 18 min, 22 cars. Official: 0 disconnects, 3.77 Hz/active car, 0 gaps. OpenF1: 10 disconnects, 2.93 Hz/car.

Events: yellows S8/S9 at 12:07:35Z (double yellow S9 12:07:42Z); car 12 ANT stopped on track 12:14:46Z (yellow S8/S9 12:14:42Z); PER/PIA impeding noted at T19; chequered 12:22:00Z. Eliminated: BOR, HUL, ALO, PER, STR, BOT. OpenF1 did **not** deliver the Q1 CHEQUERED FLAG message or the yellows at 12:21:45Z (disconnect window).

## 14. Q2 Analysis

FACT: 15 min, 15 cars. Official 0 disconnects, 3.79 Hz. OpenF1 7 disconnects, 3.03 Hz; the largest single OpenF1 hole was **29.3 s for all 22 cars (12:34:42–12:35:12Z)**.

Eliminated: BEA, LAW, ALB, OCO, LIN, ANT (no Q2 time).

## 15. Q3 Analysis

FACT: 13 min, 10 cars. Official 0 disconnects, 3.85 Hz, p50 2.17 s. OpenF1 7 disconnects, p50 14.2 s, max 28.9 s. Pole **RUS 1:42.526**; chequered 13:04:00Z; first car to take the flag HAM.

## 20. Canonical Vehicle State Feasibility

FACT: for every Position sample, CarData exists within ±240 ms for the same car, and TimingData InPit/position is available from the fold. All three join on driver number.

INFERENCE: a per-car `VehicleState {t_source, x,y,z, speed, rpm, gear, throttle, brake, in_pit, position, tyre}` is feasible from the Official feed alone at \~3.8–4.2 Hz, rendered \~3 s behind wall clock. It needs: sentinel filtering (104 → null), gear clamp (> 8 → hold last), (0,0,0) → hidden, stationary detection.

## 21. Failover Analysis

- Official: 1 disconnect all day (11:06:03Z, pre-session, recovered in 5 s). The hub previously ignored the Subscribe completion snapshot, so a mid-session reconnect would have restored only deltas — **fixed for the Race** and verified (26 topics restored on connect).
- OpenF1: 24 disconnects in Q, plus forced reconnect every \~60 min on OAuth token refresh (expect 2 in the Race).
- INFERENCE: OpenF1 is a usable fallback for \*continuity of the picture\*, but at \~14 s behind and with holes up to \~29 s. Switching sources mid-session requires matching on source timestamp (exact match proven), which makes a seamless join trivial.

Snap events (implied speed > 420 km/h): 2,705 of \~228k steps (1.2 %), all with dt 120–340 ms → timestamp jitter.

INFERENCE: interpolation between real samples (buffer \~3 s) is accurate to \~1 m typical; extrapolation into the future is 2–4× worse. Phase 15 prediction was **not** changed.

## 25. Problems and Data Quality

1. OpenF1 duplicates (50 %) — hub subscription bug (`v1/#`) — **fixed**.
2. OpenF1 disconnect every 90–135 s with 20+ s backlog; 13,051 driver-seconds missing; RCM and laps lost (e.g. BEA 1:44.775, LAW 1:44.860 best laps missing in OpenF1 — deltas 0.63 s / 0.68 s).
3. OpenF1 laps include deleted laps (BOT 1:47.147 deleted for double yellow appears as OpenF1 best).
4. Official `Status` always "OnTrack".
5. CarData sentinel 104 (\~38 % of samples) and 314 impossible gear values.
6. DRS channel absent.
7. Official SubscribeCompletion snapshot ignored by hub — **fixed**.
8. TimingData has no source time (latency unmeasurable).
9. One cached pre-session Position frame (timestamp from end of P3) at feed start.

## 26. RaceVision Recommendation

- **Official JWT = primary** for pose, telemetry, timing, race control, weather (evidence: same data, \~12 s earlier, 0 gaps, 0 disconnects, archive-identical).
- **OpenF1 = fallback** (keep; required when no JWT, and as a continuity backup).
- Render live pose \~3 s behind wall clock with interpolation, not extrapolation.
- Do not use Position `Status`; filter sentinels and bad gears in the decoder layer.
- These are recommendations only — production arbitration, providers, schema and Unity were **not** changed.

## 27. Exact Next Engineering Steps

Done tonight for the Race (test tooling only, `logs/baku_weekend_web/server.py`, gitignored, not committed):

1. Removed `v1/#` from OpenF1 subscriptions → no duplicates, half the load.
2. Handle SignalR type-3 Subscribe completion as `kind="snapshot"` → full state on every (re)connect. Verified: 26 topics restored, Position.z/CarData.z decoded, TimingData 22 lines.
3. OpenF1 MQTT callback now only enqueues; a worker thread parses/records. Each row stores true socket-arrival time plus `queue_wait_ms` and `queue_depth`; `/api/status` exposes `openf1_queue`. Self-test: 5,000 msgs, 0 lost, ordered.
4. Hub restarted: new run `20260925T191611Z`, Official connected, OpenF1 connected, Mongo OK.

Next (production, needs approval): add sentinel/gear filters to `CarDataZDecoder`; stationary detection independent of `Status`; handle SubscribeCompletion in `OfficialF1SignalRProvider` if not already; then an arbitration proposal making Official primary behind a flag.
