## What Agent 18.6 actually changed

### OpenF1 — YES, it has been created/adjusted substantially

The current OpenF1 provider now has a real MQTT streaming boundary and the 18.6 work hardened it.

The changes include:

- explicit MQTT topic subscriptions
- wildcard subscriptions such as `v1/#` rejected
- topic trimming and deduplication
- `v1/pit` and `v1/overtakes` recognised as optional topics rather than assumed defaults
- `_id` / `_key` deduplication retained
- session filtering
- source-time ordering detection
- measured latency rather than a hardcoded latency assumption
- health hysteresis
- degraded state after consecutive failures
- recovery only after a number of accepted messages
- reconnect starts in `DEGRADED`
- credentials excluded from statistics/log output

The current settings also expose the health controls:

```text
stream_degrade_after_failures
stream_recover_after_messages
stream_latency_window
```

The report explicitly says these were implemented. fileciteturn36file0L92-L100

The current repository confirms this is real code under:

```text
backend/providers/openf1/
backend/providers/openf1/streaming/
```

and the streaming settings currently include:

```text
mqtt.openf1.org
port 8883
TLS enabled
```

with streaming disabled by default.

So **OpenF1 is definitely not just a placeholder anymore.**

---

# F1 TV JWT / Official F1 — YES, but there is an important distinction

The Official F1 authentication boundary already existed, and the 18.6 work has now **adjusted the Official F1 provider around the authenticated live feed**.

The code currently contains:

```text
backend/providers/official_f1/auth/
```

with:

- `OfficialF1AuthBoundary`
- credential provider abstraction
- injected credentials
- auth state
- auth failure classification
- capability state
- entitlement models
- secret redaction
- environment configuration

The 18.6 work then hardened the actual authenticated SignalR ingestion path.

Specifically, it changed the Official provider so that:

- `Position.z` quality is enforced
- `CarData.z` quality is enforced
- `104` is treated as unavailable
- invalid pedal values are rejected
- invalid gear values are rejected
- negative RPM/speed are rejected
- DRS remains optional
- `(0,0,0)` Position is treated as no-fix
- duplicate samples are suppressed
- older source-time samples are flagged
- reconnect snapshots are treated correctly
- Subscribe completion snapshots are expanded into topic frames
- TimingData keyframes/deltas/deletions are folded
- reconnect state can be restored without duplicating samples

The report specifically states that the Official path now restores state from the Subscribe snapshot after reconnect and that Official TimingData is folded into current classification state. fileciteturn36file0L61-L70

So the answer is:

### **F1 TV JWT integration: YES**
### **Actual live F1 TV JWT race validation: NO**

That second part is extremely important.


and says:

> No credentials were used, and all transports were in-memory. fileciteturn36file0L156-L164

So Cursor has **prepared and hardened the production code**, but it has **not yet connected that code to your actual F1 TV Access JWT during a live session**.

# My recommendation for the immediate next agent

There is a subtle difference between the Agent 18.6 report's recommendation and the **best engineering order**.

The report recommends:

> **Agent 18.24 — Race-day rehearsal**, with authorised credentials, to clear RV18-0400. fileciteturn36file0L165-L166

But the Phase 18 dependency map says 18.9 and 18.23 provide important prerequisites to the full rehearsal.

So I would treat the next work as:

This should make the Official F1 and OpenF1 integrations robust against:

```text
timeouts
disconnects
reconnects
slow provider
provider unavailable
duplicate data
out-of-order data
authentication failure
token expiry

archive failure
```

Then:

### **18.9 — Performance / Load / Soak**

Test sustained telemetry ingestion against realistic race workloads.

:::writing{variant="document" id="64172" title="Agent 18.8 — Production Observability — Cursor + Manual"}
You are the Principal Distributed Systems Architect, SRE, and Senior Python Engineer for the RaceVision project.

====================================================================
RACEVISION — AGENT 18.8
PRODUCTION OBSERVABILITY
[C + M] CURSOR IMPLEMENTATION + MANUAL MONITORING VALIDATION
====================================================================

MISSION

Implement and productionize RaceVision observability across the existing
application architecture.

The objective is to make RaceVision observable during real race-day operation:

    providers
      ↓
    canonical runtime
      ↓
    persistence / EventBus
      ↓
    transport / API
      ↓
    prediction / analytics / commentary
      ↓
    Unity / OBS / streaming / webhooks

without creating a second runtime, a second metrics system, or a second source
of truth.

This agent owns:

- production metrics export
- observability aggregation
- health/readiness/liveness integration
- structured operational logging alignment
- provider/runtime/transport/API observability integration
- Prometheus-compatible metrics where appropriate
- Grafana/dashboard assets where appropriate
- observability tests
- observability documentation
- resolution of RV18-0103

This agent does NOT own:

- full load/soak testing (18.9)
- distributed tracing productionisation (18.19)
- security hardening (18.13)
- secrets rotation (18.22)
- full race-day rehearsal (18.24)
- Unity production validation (18.10)
- live OBS validation (18.11)
- redesign of the canonical runtime
- provider failover architecture

====================================================================
1. CURRENT PROJECT TRUTH
====================================================================

Repository:

kanedark316/RaceVision

Branch:

main

Read before changing anything:

docs/Roadmap.md
docs/phase18/README.md
docs/phase18/ProductionHardeningArchitecture.md
docs/phase18/MasterDefectRegister.md
docs/phase18/Phase18DependencyMap.md
docs/phase18/ProductionReadinessScorecard.md

README.md

Also inspect:

backend/monitoring/
backend/public_api/monitoring/
backend/api/observability/
backend/providers/
backend/providers/official_f1/
backend/providers/openf1/
backend/runtime/
backend/api/
backend/public_api/
shared/
infra/prometheus/
infra/grafana/
infra/nginx/
infra/docker/

Inspect tests covering:

- health
- readiness
- monitoring
- transport observability
- public API monitoring
- provider statistics
- runtime health
- deployment
- application startup

====================================================================
2. PHASE 18 DEFECT OWNERSHIP
====================================================================

This agent must explicitly address:

RV18-0103
"backend/monitoring/ architecture-only stub"

Current baseline finding:

backend/monitoring/README.md currently describes:

- health/
- metrics/
- logging/
- alerts/

but states that the package is architecture-only.

Meanwhile:

backend/public_api/monitoring/

already contains actual in-process public API metrics/monitoring.

And:

backend/api/observability/

already contains transport observability aggregation.

The final result MUST NOT leave these packages presenting contradictory
observability architectures.

Resolve RV18-0103 by establishing one coherent observability story.

Do not blindly merge everything into one giant module.

Use clear ownership boundaries.

====================================================================
3. OBSERVABILITY ARCHITECTURE
====================================================================

The intended model is:

                    ┌─────────────────────────┐
                    │   Observability Core     │
                    │ aggregation/export       │
                    └────────────┬────────────┘
                                 │
         ┌───────────────────────┼────────────────────────┐
         │                       │                        │
         ▼                       ▼                        ▼
   Provider metrics        Runtime/transport       Public API metrics
         │                       │                        │
         ├──────────────┬────────┼──────────────┬─────────┤
         ▼              ▼        ▼              ▼         ▼
      Official        OpenF1   Runtime        WS/API   Webhooks
         │              │        │              │
         └──────────────┴────────┼──────────────┴─────────┘
                                 ▼
                    Prometheus-compatible export
                                 │
                                 ▼
                              Grafana
                                 │
                                 ▼
                         Operator / race-day

Health endpoints remain operational health/readiness surfaces.

Metrics remain observational.

Logs remain event records.

Tracing remains a separate Phase 18.19 concern.

====================================================================
4. NON-NEGOTIABLE ARCHITECTURAL INVARIANTS
====================================================================

1. One canonical runtime.
2. Phase 10 remains race-state authority.
3. Observability never mutates canonical runtime state.
4. Metrics must be observational only.
5. Metrics exporters must not block telemetry ingestion.
6. Metrics failures must not crash RaceVision.
7. Health/readiness must not become a second runtime.
8. Providers must remain provider adapters.
9. Unity must remain a consumer.
10. OBS must remain an output boundary.
11. Public API remains an access boundary.
12. Prediction remains derived.
13. Analytics remains derived.
14. Commentary remains derived.
15. No credential/token/password may appear in metrics or logs.
16. No raw provider payload may appear in metrics.
17. No unbounded/high-cardinality identifiers may become Prometheus labels.
18. Session/driver IDs must not be used as arbitrary metric labels unless there
    is an explicit bounded design justification.
19. Correlation IDs must not become metric labels.
20. Raw URLs must not become metric labels.
21. Receive time must not replace source time in telemetry semantics.
22. Provider telemetry availability must be observable independently from
    application readiness.
23. Lack of an active live F1 session must NOT make the whole application
    unhealthy.
24. Observability must work in offline/unit-test mode.
25. Manual live monitoring validation must be clearly distinguished from
    automated tests.

====================================================================
5. REUSE EXISTING OBSERVABILITY
====================================================================

Before implementing anything, understand and reuse:

A. backend/public_api/monitoring/

Existing metrics include categories such as:

REST:
- requests
- errors
- duration
- bounded route/status/version buckets

Auth:
- failures
- authorization denials

Rate limiting:
- limited requests

WebSocket:
- attempts
- connections
- disconnects
- auth failures
- subscriptions
- messages
- active connections
- active subscriptions

Webhooks:
- deliveries
- retries
- failures
- duration

Streaming:
- operations
- duration

B. backend/api/observability/

Existing transport observability includes:

- transport state
- connections
- subscriptions
- streaming
- outbound
- security
- rate limit
- heartbeat
- commands
- errors
- health

C. Official F1 provider statistics

Integrate existing provider statistics such as:

- accepted Position samples
- accepted CarData samples
- TimingData
- duplicate samples
- out-of-order samples
- session mismatches
- no-fix position samples
- sentinel channels
- invalid channels
- topic observations
- reconnect/snapshot behaviour
- timing fold counters
- other existing safe provider statistics

D. OpenF1 statistics

Integrate safe statistics such as:

- state
- subscribed topics
- messages received
- filtered messages
- decode failures
- auth failures
- reconnect count
- out-of-order samples
- latency summary
- buffer depth
- dedupe statistics
- last source timestamp
- last receive timestamp
- token SAFE status only

NEVER export actual token contents.

====================================================================
6. PRODUCTION OBSERVABILITY CATEGORIES
====================================================================

Create a coherent metrics taxonomy.

At minimum, evaluate the following categories against the existing code and
implement the useful subset that is justified.

------------------------------------------------------------
A. APPLICATION / PROCESS
------------------------------------------------------------

Examples:

racevision_build_info
racevision_process_uptime_seconds

Also expose safe application/runtime identity metadata where bounded and useful.

Do not export secrets.

------------------------------------------------------------
B. APPLICATION HEALTH
------------------------------------------------------------

Represent:

- liveness
- readiness
- degraded state
- dependency health
- startup state
- database readiness where already supported
- runtime availability

Health must distinguish:

LIVE SESSION ABSENT

from:

APPLICATION BROKEN

For example:

No current F1 session
    ≠
database unavailable
    ≠
provider disconnected
    ≠
application process dead

------------------------------------------------------------
C. DATABASE / PERSISTENCE
------------------------------------------------------------

Where existing health/metrics allow it, expose:

- database availability
- connection health
- query/error counters already available
- persistence failures
- writer retry counts
- queue/backlog indicators already present
- migration/startup state where appropriate

Do not add expensive query instrumentation just for this agent.

Do not create fake database metrics.

------------------------------------------------------------
D. RUNTIME
------------------------------------------------------------

Where existing metrics can support it:

- runtime state
- current mode
- live/replay state
- session availability
- snapshot freshness
- EventBus activity
- runtime errors
- accepted frames
- dropped/rejected frames
- timing regressions
- interpolation usage
- prediction usage where safe

Do not turn large runtime history into unbounded metrics.

------------------------------------------------------------
E. OFFICIAL F1 PROVIDER
------------------------------------------------------------

Expose safe, operationally useful metrics such as:

- provider state
- connection state
- reconnect count
- decode failures
- accepted samples
- duplicate samples
- out-of-order samples
- session mismatches
- no-fix position samples
- sentinel channel counts
- invalid channel counts
- TimingData fold activity
- last source timestamp age
- last receive timestamp age
- measured latency summary where available
- topic activity / observed topic state
- subscription state where available

The exporter must not expose:

- JWT
- F1 TV credentials
- authorization headers
- token strings
- cookies
- raw payloads

------------------------------------------------------------
F. OPENF1
------------------------------------------------------------

Expose safe operational metrics such as:

- connection state
- reconnect count
- authentication failures
- decode failures
- filtered-out messages
- duplicate count
- out-of-order count
- accepted messages
- buffer depth
- latency p50
- latency p95
- latency max where useful
- last source timestamp age
- last received timestamp age
- current subscribed topic count

Do not expose:

- username
- password
- access token
- raw MQTT credentials

Safe token status may be reduced to booleans/counters such as:

has_token
needs_refresh
refresh_count

only if the existing design makes them useful.

------------------------------------------------------------
G. TRANSPORT
------------------------------------------------------------

Reuse Agent 11.12 observability.

Expose where useful:

- active WebSockets
- connections
- disconnects
- subscriptions
- outbound pressure
- transport errors
- command counts
- heartbeat/connection state

Do not duplicate transport counting logic if an existing aggregation can be
exported directly.

------------------------------------------------------------
H. PUBLIC API
------------------------------------------------------------

Reuse Agent 17.11 monitoring.

Expose:

- request totals
- 4xx/5xx totals
- request duration
- auth failures
- authz denials
- rate-limit denials
- WebSocket counts
- webhook delivery outcomes
- stream operation outcomes

Labels MUST remain bounded.

------------------------------------------------------------
I. WEBHOOKS
------------------------------------------------------------

Expose:

- delivery attempts
- successful delivery
- retry
- timeout
- permanent failure
- queue full
- inbound accepted
- inbound duplicate
- inbound rejected if those counters already exist

Do not export:

- HMAC secret
- signed payload
- provider secret
- full payload
- arbitrary event IDs as labels

------------------------------------------------------------
J. TELEMETRY QUALITY
------------------------------------------------------------

Use the existing provider quality counters.

Useful examples:

racevision_telemetry_samples_total
racevision_telemetry_duplicates_total
racevision_telemetry_out_of_order_total
racevision_telemetry_decode_failures_total
racevision_telemetry_unavailable_channels_total
racevision_telemetry_no_fix_total

Do not invent counts that the runtime does not actually know.

------------------------------------------------------------
K. LATENCY
------------------------------------------------------------

Where safe, represent measured latency.

Official F1:

- source → received latency
- current sample age

OpenF1:

- source → received latency
- p50
- p95
- max where safe

DO NOT hardcode Baku latency as a production metric.

Baku values are evidence, not SLA.

============================================================
7. PROMETHEUS EXPORT
============================================================

Implement a production-safe metrics export mechanism.

Prefer an established Prometheus-compatible implementation already accepted by
the repository dependency policy.

If adding a dependency is necessary, inspect pyproject.toml and dependency
conventions first.

Do not add a dependency merely for convenience if existing code can safely
support the export contract.

The exporter must:

- be read-only
- be fast
- be bounded
- be safe under concurrent reads
- tolerate absent optional components
- never leak secrets
- never block ingestion
- never throw into the main runtime
- expose deterministic metric names
- expose deterministic label sets
- use appropriate counter/gauge/histogram semantics

Choose metric types correctly.

Counters for cumulative events.

Gauges for current state.

Histograms for request/latency distributions only where instrumentation already
supports useful observations.

Do not pretend that p50/p95 histograms exist if they were never collected.

====================================================================
8. LABEL / CARDINALITY POLICY
====================================================================

This is mandatory.

Allowed bounded labels may include:

provider
feed
transport
operation
outcome
status_class
api_version
component

Avoid:

session_id
session_key
driver_id
correlation_id
request_id
raw_url
exception_text
payload
message_body
credentials

unless there is a very strong bounded justification.

Never use arbitrary exception strings as labels.

Never use user-controlled strings as labels.

Never use raw provider topic/payload data as labels without bounding it.

Document the cardinality policy.

====================================================================
9. METRICS ENDPOINT SECURITY
====================================================================

Determine whether the metrics endpoint is:

- internal-only
- authenticated
- localhost/internal network
- separately protected by reverse proxy

Do not expose production metrics publicly without an explicit reason.

Metrics can reveal:

- traffic patterns
- provider health
- endpoint usage
- internal structure

Therefore the exporter must respect the existing security architecture.

Do not bypass Public API authentication casually.

Do not expose credentials or secrets through metrics.

If an internal scrape endpoint is needed, document the deployment assumption.

Do not invent a deployment firewall configuration without evidence.

====================================================================
10. HEALTH / READINESS / LIVENESS
====================================================================

Audit existing health endpoints.

Establish clear semantics:

LIVENESS

Means:

Process is running sufficiently to answer the liveness request.

READINESS

Means:

The application is ready for the configured deployment role.

A missing live F1 session must not necessarily make readiness fail.

Provider health should be reported as dependency/runtime state separately.

Examples:

application_ready = true
official_f1 = disconnected
openf1 = disabled
live_session = absent

This may still be an operationally valid state.

But:

database unavailable
startup incomplete
critical internal dependency broken

may make readiness fail depending on existing production profile semantics.

Reuse the current `RV_PROFILE=production` readiness contract.

Do not break the production database readiness work from Agent 18.5/18.6.

====================================================================
11. STRUCTURED LOGGING
====================================================================

Audit current logging conventions.

Create/reuse structured operational events for:

- provider connected
- provider disconnected
- reconnect
- authentication failure
- degradation
- recovery
- runtime state change
- readiness change
- persistence failure
- webhook failure
- export failure

Do not log:

- passwords
- JWTs
- OAuth tokens
- authorization headers
- cookies
- signed webhook payloads
- raw high-volume telemetry payloads
- full stack traces on every ordinary telemetry failure

Use existing redaction helpers where available.

Metrics and logging should complement each other.

Do not duplicate the entire payload into both.

====================================================================
12. PROVIDER-SPECIFIC OBSERVABILITY
====================================================================

Official F1:

Use existing OfficialF1RuntimeIngestStats and quality observer mechanisms.

Make them observable through the production observability boundary without
changing canonical provider semantics.

OpenF1:

Use existing stream_statistics() and latency_summary().

Do not alter the OpenF1 streaming protocol simply to make metrics easier.

Do not expose raw token data.

Do not change source-time semantics.

====================================================================
13. OFFICIAL F1 / OPENF1 RELATIONSHIP
====================================================================

Observability must make it possible for an operator to answer:

- Is Official F1 connected?
- Is Official F1 authenticated?
- Is Position.z arriving?
- Is CarData.z arriving?
- Are samples being rejected?
- Are sentinels increasing?
- Are duplicates increasing?
- Are source timestamps advancing?
- Is latency increasing?
- Has Official F1 disconnected?
- Is OpenF1 connected?
- Is OpenF1 degraded?
- Is OpenF1 receiving samples?
- How much buffering is occurring?
- Is OpenF1 latency increasing?

But this agent MUST NOT implement automatic Official → OpenF1 arbitration.

Provider arbitration remains a separate concern.

Observability reports the truth.

It does not decide provider selection.

Do not redesign Phase 15/16.

Do not add provider-aware logic to analytics.

====================================================================

RaceVision observability exists to tell operators what the system is actually
doing.

It must never make the system appear healthier than it is.

Therefore:

observed > inferred
bounded > unbounded
safe > convenient
one observability architecture > duplicated monitoring systems

Most importantly:

OFFICIAL F1 + F1 TV JWT
and
OPENF1 MQTT

must be observable without exposing their credentials.

Observability reports provider health.

It does not become provider arbitration.

## Manual part after Cursor finishes

The manual side is important because **18.8 is `[C+M]`**. Cursor can implement the exporter and dashboard, but it cannot prove your actual monitoring platform can scrape and alert on the deployed application.

Use Cursor's final report to get the **exact metrics endpoint** it implemented rather than assuming a route.

### Manual validation order

```text
1. Run RaceVision production-like
2. Verify liveness
3. Verify readiness
4. Verify metrics endpoint
5. Start Prometheus
6. Confirm RaceVision target = UP
7. Generate API traffic
8. Confirm counters change
9. Exercise WebSocket
10. Exercise provider test/simulation
11. Check Official F1 metrics
12. Check OpenF1 metrics
13. Stop/recover a dependency
14. Confirm degraded/recovered state
15. Open Grafana
16. Import/load RaceVision dashboard
17. Confirm live panels
18. Perform secret scan
```

### What you should specifically check for your F1 integrations

For **Official F1**, the dashboard should let you see things such as:

```text
CONNECTED / DEGRADED / FAILED
Position.z samples
CarData.z samples
TimingData activity
decode failures
duplicate samples
out-of-order samples
104/sentinel counts
(0,0,0) no-fix counts
source freshness
measured latency
reconnect count
```

For **OpenF1**:

```text
HEALTHY / DEGRADED / FAILED
messages received
filtered messages
decode failures
duplicates
out-of-order
buffer depth
latency p50
latency p95
last source timestamp
reconnect count
token refresh count
```

The last item must only show safe token metadata, never the token itself. The current OpenF1 implementation already has a `safe_status()` design for this purpose.

### One particularly important manual test

During the validation, deliberately make a provider unhealthy.

You want to see something like:

```text
Official F1
HEALTHY
   ↓
DEGRADED
   ↓
FAILED
```

and later:

```text
FAILED
   ↓
RECOVERING
   ↓
HEALTHY
```

**without the observability system itself crashing or changing canonical race state.**

That is exactly the kind of evidence we need before moving deeper into 18.9/18.23/18.24.

### Current Phase 18 position

I would therefore have Cursor handle **18.8 now**, and only after its report comes back decide whether 18.9, 18.12, 18.13/18.22, or another dependency should be next. The Phase 18 baseline identifies `RV18-0303` — production metrics collector absent — as an explicit 18.8-owned gap, and `RV18-0103` as the contradictory monitoring architecture that this agent should resolve. fileciteturn36file0L79-L99

### What you need to do

Run the following from your **RaceVision repo root** in Cursor's PowerShell terminal.

#### 1. Start RaceVision in production profile

Use the same entry point the agent validated against:

```powershell
$env:RV_PROFILE="production"
python -m backend.production
```

Leave that terminal running.

The report confirms the entry point is `python -m backend.production` and that production-like profiles require the production database/readiness conditions. fileciteturn103file0L78-L82

---

#### 2. Test liveness

Open a **second** PowerShell terminal.

The exact liveness URL is documented in the new `backend/monitoring/README.md`. Rather than guessing the route, in Cursor open:

```text
backend/monitoring/README.md
```

Find the manual validation section and use the exact URL shown there.

Expected result: **HTTP 200 / healthy response**.

Important: liveness should still work even if the database or F1 providers are unavailable. fileciteturn103file0L78-L81

---

#### 3. Test readiness

Use the exact readiness URL from the same README.

Expected result in the normal production setup: **ready/HTTP 200**.

Readiness is different from liveness: production-like profiles check that the runtime is wired and the database is reachable with migrations applied. fileciteturn103file0L80-L82

---

#### 4. Test the metrics endpoint

The new endpoint is:

```text
GET /api/v1/metrics
```

The agent confirms this endpoint is implemented and producing Prometheus text successfully. fileciteturn103file0L59-L63

In PowerShell:

```powershell
Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
```

You should get **HTTP 200** and a large text response containing metric names.

To inspect it more easily:

```powershell
(Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics).Content
```

Look for things such as:

```text
racevision_
```

and:

```text
racevision_observability_collector_up
```

---

### 5. Test Prometheus

This is the first part that requires an actual monitoring system.

The agent created:

```text
infra/prometheus/prometheus.example.yml
infra/prometheus/racevision-alerts.yml
```

The example configuration uses a **15-second scrape interval**, a **10-second timeout**, and expects the RaceVision target to be configured rather than assuming a particular deployment environment. fileciteturn103file0L112-L119

You need to:

1. Copy the example Prometheus configuration.
2. Configure its RaceVision target to your running backend.
3. Start Prometheus.
4. Open the Prometheus UI.
5. Go to **Status → Targets**.
6. Confirm the RaceVision target says:

```text
UP
```

That is a critical manual check because the agent specifically reports that **Prometheus target UP has not yet been tested**. fileciteturn103file0L158-L162

---

### 6. Check that metrics actually change

With Prometheus scraping RaceVision, generate some API traffic.

For example:

```powershell
Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
```

and exercise a couple of normal API endpoints.

Then in Prometheus query the exported metrics.

You are checking that the counters change, rather than merely verifying that `/metrics` returns text.

The exporter includes metrics for runtime, transport, public API, webhooks, Official F1 and OpenF1. fileciteturn103file0L72-L77 fileciteturn103file0L105-L110

---

### 7. Check WebSocket metrics

Start/use one RaceVision WebSocket connection and then look at the corresponding transport metrics in Prometheus.

The goal is to demonstrate that:

```text
WebSocket connection
        ↓
RaceVision transport
        ↓
monitoring counters
        ↓
/api/v1/metrics
        ↓
Prometheus
```

The agent specifically lists **WebSocket counters** as a manual validation item that has not yet been executed. fileciteturn103file0L158-L161

---

### 8. Check Official F1 / OpenF1 provider state

This one depends on having the providers configured.

You are looking for provider metrics showing their connection/lifecycle state.

The exporter includes Official F1 lifecycle, connection, authentication state, quality and feed counters, while OpenF1 includes connection state, reconnects, deduplication, malformed messages, buffer depth and measured latency. fileciteturn103file0L83-L103

You **do not need to expose credentials**. The implementation deliberately avoids exporting tokens/passwords. fileciteturn103file0L102-L103

---

### 9. Test a failure and recovery

This is important.

Stop or make one dependency unavailable, then observe:

```text
Healthy
   ↓
Dependency failure
   ↓
Degraded/unhealthy metric state
   ↓
Dependency restored
   ↓
Recovery
```

At minimum, the report expects database failure/recovery and provider degradation to be tested. fileciteturn103file0L154-L162

Do **not** deliberately corrupt your database. A controlled stop/start of the local database service is sufficient.

---

### 10. Open Grafana

The agent created:

```text
infra/grafana/dashboards/racevision-operations.json
```

It contains **43 panels across five rows** covering:

- Overview
- Official F1
- OpenF1
- Transport / Public API / Webhooks
- Alerts / Attention fileciteturn103file0L127-L128

Import the dashboard into your Grafana instance and configure its Prometheus datasource.

Then verify that the dashboard actually displays live RaceVision data.

The agent explicitly says **importing the dashboard into Grafana has not been run yet**. fileciteturn103file0L188-L192

---

### 11. Check secret exposure

With RaceVision running, inspect:

```text
/api/v1/metrics
```

and your normal application logs.

Make sure you do **not** see:

```text
JWT tokens
MQTT passwords
OpenF1 tokens
API credentials
authorization headers
```

The implementation has tests for this, but the manual step is to prove nothing unexpected appears in the running system. The report says the live-output secret scan has not yet been executed. fileciteturn103file0L133-L137

---

## The easiest way for you to do this

I would **not** try to figure out every command yourself.

Open this file in Cursor:

```text
backend/monitoring/README.md
```

The Agent 18.8 report says the **PowerShell startup steps and the complete 14-item manual checklist are already there**. fileciteturn103file0L158-L162

Run them in this order:

```text
1. Start RaceVision
2. Liveness
3. Readiness
4. /api/v1/metrics
5. Start Prometheus
6. Prometheus target = UP
7. Generate API traffic
8. Check API metrics
9. Check WebSocket metrics
10. Check Official F1/OpenF1 state
11. Simulate dependency failure
12. Restore dependency
13. Import/check Grafana dashboard
14. Check logs/metrics for secrets
```

### What counts as success?

You don't need to make every possible production scenario work. You need **evidence** for each manual check: HTTP response, Prometheus target status, visible metrics, Grafana data, and failure/recovery behaviour.

Once those checks pass, you can give Cursor the evidence and have it update:

```text
RV18-0303
Phase 18.8 status
Roadmap
MasterDefectRegister
```

The report currently says **RV18-0103 is resolved**, but **RV18-0303 remains OPEN specifically because nothing has yet been scraped in staging/manual validation**. fileciteturn103file0L164-L169

**I would do the first four checks first, then Prometheus, then Grafana.** That avoids setting up Grafana before we know RaceVision itself is exposing the metrics correctly.

Below is the **full manual runbook**, in the order I recommend doing it on your Windows machine.

# Part A — Prometheus

## 1. Open the RaceVision repo in Cursor

Open your RaceVision repository in Cursor.

Open a PowerShell terminal at the repository root.

Verify:

```powershell
Get-Location
```

Then:

```powershell
Get-ChildItem .\backend\monitoring
Get-ChildItem .\infra\prometheus
```

You should see the new monitoring package and the Prometheus files created by Agent 18.8.

The important files are:

```text
backend/monitoring/README.md
infra/prometheus/prometheus.example.yml
infra/prometheus/racevision-alerts.yml
```

The agent reports those files were created as part of 18.8. fileciteturn103file0L170-L185

---

## 2. Read the exact manual instructions created by Agent 18.8

Run:

```powershell
code .\backend\monitoring\README.md
```

Or simply open the file in Cursor.

Then search for:

```text
Manual Validation
```

and:

```text
PowerShell
```

The Agent 18.8 report specifically says that the README contains the **PowerShell startup instructions and the 14-item manual checklist**. fileciteturn103file0L154-L162

Do not skip this because it contains the exact project-specific settings.

---

# 3. Check Docker Desktop

For the easiest Prometheus/Grafana setup, I recommend using Docker Desktop.

Run:

```powershell
docker --version
```

Then:

```powershell
docker compose version
```

Then:

```powershell
docker info
```

### Expected

You should get Docker version information and `docker info` should return successfully.

If `docker info` fails, **do not continue with Prometheus/Grafana yet**. Start Docker Desktop and repeat the command.

---

# 4. Check whether anything is already using ports 8000, 9090 and 3000

RaceVision uses port 8000 in the current deployment path, and the agent's manual instructions reference that port. fileciteturn103file0L37-L39

Run:

```powershell
Get-NetTCPConnection -LocalPort 8000,9090,3000 -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
    Select-Object LocalPort,State,OwningProcess
```

You ideally want:

```text
8000  → available for RaceVision
9090  → available for Prometheus
3000  → available for Grafana
```

If 9090 or 3000 is already occupied, stop the old container later rather than starting another one on the same port.

---

# 5. Start RaceVision

Open **Terminal 1**.

From the RaceVision repo root:

```powershell
$env:RV_PROFILE="production"
python -m backend.production
```

Leave this terminal running.

Do **not** close it.

You are now testing the same production entry point referenced by the Agent 18.8 work. fileciteturn103file0L37-L39

---

# 6. Test RaceVision itself before starting Prometheus

Open **Terminal 2**.

First test the metrics endpoint:

```powershell
$r = Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
$r.StatusCode
```

Expected:

```text
200
```

Then:

```powershell
$r.Content.Substring(0,[Math]::Min(3000,$r.Content.Length))
```

You should see Prometheus-style metrics text.

The agent specifically confirms the endpoint is:

```text
GET /api/v1/metrics
```

and that it works end-to-end. fileciteturn103file0L59-L63

---

# 7. Check that the exporter is really returning RaceVision metrics

Run:

```powershell
($r.Content -split "`n") |
    Where-Object { $_ -match "^racevision_" } |
    Select-Object -First 50
```

You should see lines beginning with:

```text
racevision_
```

Also check:

```powershell
($r.Content -split "`n") |
    Where-Object { $_ -match "racevision_observability_collector_up" }
```

The agent explicitly reports the collector-health metric as:

```text
racevision_observability_collector_up{collector}=0/1
```

with failed collectors isolated from the overall scrape. fileciteturn103file0L69-L77

---

# 8. Test liveness

Now use the **exact liveness route documented in `backend/monitoring/README.md`**.

You can first find it from PowerShell:

```powershell
Select-String -Path .\backend\monitoring\README.md `
    -Pattern "liveness","readiness","metrics" `
    -Context 2,4
```

Use the URL shown there.

The important expected behaviour is:

**Liveness should remain independent of the database and F1 providers.** fileciteturn103file0L78-L81

---

# 9. Test readiness

Use the exact readiness URL shown by the project's monitoring README.

Expected in your correctly configured production environment:

```text
HTTP 200
```

The Agent 18.8 design says production-like readiness requires:

```text
Runtime wired
+
Database reachable
+
Migrations applied
```

but a missing live session or degraded provider should **not** make the application unready. fileciteturn103file0L78-L82

---

# 10. Prepare a temporary Prometheus directory

We don't need to modify RaceVision's architecture just to do this manual test.

Run:

```powershell
$MonitorHome = Join-Path $HOME "RaceVision-Monitoring"
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force $MonitorHome | Out-Null
```

Now copy the files:

```powershell
Copy-Item .\infra\prometheus\prometheus.example.yml `
    (Join-Path $MonitorHome "prometheus.yml") -Force

Copy-Item .\infra\prometheus\racevision-alerts.yml `
    (Join-Path $MonitorHome "racevision-alerts.yml") -Force
```

Verify:

```powershell
Get-ChildItem $MonitorHome
```

You should have:

```text
prometheus.yml
racevision-alerts.yml
```

---

# 11. Inspect the Prometheus configuration before starting Prometheus

Run:

```powershell
Get-Content (Join-Path $MonitorHome "prometheus.yml")
```

Look carefully at:

```text
scrape_configs
```

and the RaceVision target.

The Agent 18.8 config was deliberately created with:

- 15-second scrape interval
- 10-second timeout
- an explicit RaceVision target placeholder
- bearer credentials read from a file when authentication is configured. fileciteturn103file0L112-L119

### Very important

Do not blindly replace configuration values without checking the actual file.

Look for:

```text
RACEVISION_BACKEND_HOST
```

and any:

```text
credentials_file
```

entry.

---

# 12. For your first local test, make sure the metrics endpoint is reachable

The Agent 18.8 implementation supports an explicit:

```text
METRICS_ALLOW_UNAUTHENTICATED=true
```

opt-in. In production-like profiles, otherwise a configured metrics token is expected. fileciteturn103file0L121-L126

For the **initial local smoke test**, you can explicitly enable the open endpoint.

Stop RaceVision with:

```text
Ctrl+C
```

Then restart it in Terminal 1:

```powershell
$env:RV_PROFILE="production"
$env:METRICS_ALLOW_UNAUTHENTICATED="true"
python -m backend.production
```

Then repeat:

```powershell
Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
```

Expected:

```text
200
```

### Important

This is for the local validation test only. It is **not** the configuration I would use for your eventual public production deployment.

The agent deliberately supports both authenticated scraping and explicit unauthenticated opt-in. fileciteturn103file0L121-L126

---

# 13. Make the Prometheus target point to your Windows host

Because Prometheus will run inside Docker, `127.0.0.1` from inside the Prometheus container means the **Prometheus container itself**, not your Windows host.

For the Docker test, the host target should therefore resolve to:

```text
host.docker.internal:8000
```

Use the existing RaceVision example file as the starting point and change only the target host as required by the actual configuration.

After editing, check:

```powershell
Get-Content (Join-Path $MonitorHome "prometheus.yml")
```

Make sure you have not accidentally introduced invalid YAML.

---

# 14. Start Prometheus

Run:

```powershell
docker rm -f racevision-prometheus 2>$null
```

Then:

```powershell
docker run -d `
  --name racevision-prometheus `
  -p 9090:9090 `
  -v "${MonitorHome}:/etc/prometheus:ro" `
  prom/prometheus
```

Check:

```powershell
docker ps --filter "name=racevision-prometheus"
```

Expected:

```text
racevision-prometheus
Up
```

---

# 15. Check Prometheus logs immediately

Run:

```powershell
docker logs racevision-prometheus
```

Look for configuration errors.

You are looking for a clean startup rather than errors such as:

```text
yaml parse error
file not found
connection refused
invalid configuration
```

A quick way to inspect the latest output:

```powershell
docker logs --tail 100 racevision-prometheus
```

---

# 16. Open Prometheus

Open this in your browser:

```text
http://localhost:9090
```

You should see the Prometheus interface.

Then go to:

```text
Status
→ Targets
```

This is the **key manual validation point**.

The RaceVision target must show:

```text
UP
```

The Agent 18.8 report explicitly identifies **Prometheus target UP** as a manual check that was not executed yet. fileciteturn103file0L154-L162

---

# 17. If the target says DOWN

Do not immediately change RaceVision code.

In Prometheus:

```text
Status → Targets
```

Click the RaceVision target and inspect the error.

Common possibilities:

```text
connection refused
```

means RaceVision is not listening on the expected host/port.

```text
404
```

means the metrics path is wrong.

```text
403
```

means the metrics endpoint requires authentication.

```text
context deadline exceeded
```

means Prometheus cannot reach RaceVision.

Go back and verify:

```powershell
Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
```

works from Windows first.

---

# 18. Query the Prometheus `up` metric

In Prometheus, go to:

```text
Graph
```

Run:

```text
up
```

You should get a result corresponding to RaceVision.

Expected value:

```text
1
```

A value of:

```text
0
```

means Prometheus knows about the target but cannot successfully scrape it.

---

# 19. Query RaceVision's collector health

Run:

```text
racevision_observability_collector_up
```

You should see the collectors.

You are looking for values of:

```text
1
```

for healthy collectors.

This is important because Agent 18.8 implemented isolation so one collector failing should not destroy the entire `/metrics` scrape. fileciteturn103file0L69-L70

---

# 20. Confirm the metrics are being refreshed

Wait at least one scrape interval — the configuration uses a 15-second interval. fileciteturn103file0L112-L118

Refresh the RaceVision metrics and then look in Prometheus under:

```text
Graph
```

The important thing is that Prometheus is obtaining **new samples**, not just that the first scrape succeeded.

---

# 21. Generate normal API traffic

With RaceVision still running, in PowerShell run several requests:

```powershell
1..20 | ForEach-Object {
    try {
        Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics | Out-Null
    } catch {}
}
```

Then return to Prometheus.

The exporter has public API request/error metrics, and the implementation specifically tested bounded route labels. fileciteturn103file0L105-L108

You want to see that your traffic is represented in the exported metrics.

---

# 22. Test the WebSocket side

The Agent 18.8 report says WebSocket counters are part of the manual validation matrix. fileciteturn103file0L158-L162

Use the project's documented WebSocket endpoint from the RaceVision API documentation.

Create one legitimate WebSocket connection using the normal RaceVision client/test path.

Then check:

```text
/api/v1/metrics
```

again.

You should see the relevant transport/WebSocket counters change.

Don't fabricate metric names here; use the catalogue in:

```text
backend/monitoring/README.md
```

---

# 23. Check Official F1 metrics

If your Official F1 provider is configured and running, inspect:

```text
/api/v1/metrics
```

for the Official F1 metric families.

The exporter currently includes:

```text
lifecycle state
connection state
last error category
authentication state
data quality
per-feed quality
sample counts
decode failures
duplicate counts
out-of-order counts
TimingData fold operations
observed cars
receive timestamps
```

and deliberately does not expose credentials. fileciteturn103file0L83-L92

---

# 24. Check OpenF1 metrics

If OpenF1 is configured/running, inspect the OpenF1 series.

The exporter includes:

```text
stream state
connection state
reconnects
deduplication
malformed records
buffer depth
latency p50
latency p95
latency max
token presence/refresh metadata
```

while not exposing the credentials themselves. fileciteturn103file0L94-L103

---

# 25. Test database failure/recovery

This is one of the important manual checks in the 18.8 matrix. fileciteturn103file0L154-L162

First determine your PostgreSQL service name:

```powershell
Get-Service *postgres* | Select-Object Name,Status,DisplayName
```

Only if RaceVision is using your local PostgreSQL service, stop the appropriate PostgreSQL 17 service.

For example, if the returned service is actually named:

```text
postgresql-x64-17
```

then:

```powershell
Stop-Service postgresql-x64-17
```

Do **not** use that exact service name unless `Get-Service` shows it.

Now check RaceVision:

```powershell
Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
```

Then inspect readiness/metrics.

You are checking that:

```text
database failure
≠
application process failure
```

and that observability still functions.

---

# 26. Restore PostgreSQL

Start the actual service name you discovered:

```powershell
Start-Service postgresql-x64-17
```

Then verify:

```powershell
Get-Service *postgres* | Select-Object Name,Status
```

You want:

```text
Running
```

Wait through the readiness cache window. Agent 18.8 says readiness is cached for **10 seconds**. fileciteturn103file0L80-L82

Then recheck readiness and metrics.

You want evidence of recovery.

---

# 27. Check Prometheus still scrapes after recovery

Return to:

```text
http://localhost:9090
```

Then:

```text
Status → Targets
```

The RaceVision target should remain:

```text
UP
```

and the RaceVision metrics should continue arriving.

---

# 28. Check Prometheus alert rules

In Prometheus go to:

```text
Alerts
```

The Agent 18.8 implementation added:

```text
infra/prometheus/racevision-alerts.yml
```

with 12 rules. The report says those rules are based on metrics the backend actually exports and the thresholds are operational starting points, **not SLOs**. fileciteturn103file0L112-L119

Check whether the rules have loaded.

You do **not** need to artificially trigger every alert just to pass 18.8.

---

# Part B — Grafana

Once Prometheus is definitely showing RaceVision as `UP`, move to Grafana.

# 29. Start Grafana

First remove an old test container if one exists:

```powershell
docker rm -f racevision-grafana 2>$null
```

Then:

```powershell
docker run -d `
  --name racevision-grafana `
  -p 3000:3000 `

```


```text
Up
```

# 30. Open Grafana

Open:

```text
http://localhost:3000
```

Grafana should load.

Use the credentials shown by the Grafana version/container startup if it presents an initial sign-in/setup flow.

Do not put Grafana credentials into RaceVision files or Git.

---

# 31. Add Prometheus as the Grafana data source

Inside Grafana:

```text
Connections
→ Data sources
→ Add data source
→ Prometheus
```

For a local Docker setup, use the Prometheus container's network-accessible URL.

The simplest approach is to put both containers onto the same Docker network.

Create a network:

```powershell
docker network create racevision-monitoring 2>$null
```

Connect the existing containers:

```powershell
docker network connect racevision-monitoring racevision-prometheus 2>$null
docker network connect racevision-monitoring racevision-grafana 2>$null
```

Then the Prometheus URL from Grafana can use:

```text
http://racevision-prometheus:9090
```

Save and test.

### Expected

Grafana should report something equivalent to:

```text
Data source is working
```

---

# 32. Import the RaceVision dashboard

The Agent 18.8 report says the dashboard is:

```text
infra/grafana/dashboards/racevision-operations.json
```

and contains:

```text
43 panels
5 rows
```

covering:

```text
Overview
Official F1
OpenF1
Transport/Public API/Webhooks
Alerts/Attention
```

fileciteturn103file0L127-L128

In Grafana:

```text
Dashboards
→ New
→ Import dashboard
```

Choose:

and select:

```text
RaceVision\infra\grafana\dashboards\racevision-operations.json
```

During dashboard import, Grafana may ask you to select a datasource.

Choose the Prometheus datasource you created.

Then complete the import.

# 34. Open the RaceVision Operations dashboard

You should now see the RaceVision operations dashboard.

Verify the five major sections/rows:

```text
Overview
Official F1
OpenF1

Alerts / Attention
```

The report states that these are already represented in the dashboard. fileciteturn103file0L127-L128

---

This is crucial.

A dashboard importing successfully is **not enough**.

Look for panels that show:

```text
No data
```

versus actual values.

At least the overview/health panels should be populated when their corresponding RaceVision components are active.

Then generate some API requests again:

```powershell
    } catch {}
}
```

Return to Grafana and refresh the dashboard.

You want the panels to reflect activity.

---

# 36. Check the Official F1 row

If Official F1 is running, verify that the relevant panels show actual provider state rather than permanently blank values.

The metrics cover connection lifecycle, authentication state and feed quality. fileciteturn103file0L83-L92

Do not treat an empty panel as a code failure if the provider itself is not currently running.

Record the actual state.

---

# 37. Check the OpenF1 row

If OpenF1 is enabled, inspect:

```text
connection
reconnects
dedupe
buffer
latency
token refresh metadata
```

The agent specifically implemented those observability values. fileciteturn103file0L94-L103

Again, an inactive provider can legitimately show no live data.

# 38. Check Transport / API / Webhook panels

Generate some API traffic and refresh Grafana.

Then verify the transport/public API sections respond.

The agent says public API route labels are bounded and anything that doesn't match a registered template is labelled `unmatched`; a 43-request test confirmed the labels remain bounded. fileciteturn103file0L105-L108

---

# 39. Test the alert/attention area

Open:

```text
Alerts / Attention
```

Check that Grafana can query the Prometheus alert-related metrics/rules.

# 40. Test failure/recovery visually

Now repeat the database failure test from Step 25.

While the database is unavailable:

```text
RaceVision
    ↓
metrics
    ↓
Prometheus
    ↓
Grafana
```

Watch the relevant health/attention information.

Then restore PostgreSQL.

Wait for the 10-second readiness cache to expire and refresh Grafana.

You should be able to see the state recover.

# 41. Check logs for credentials

Back in PowerShell:

```powershell

```

and:

```powershell

```

Then inspect RaceVision's Terminal 1 logs.

You are looking for accidental exposure of:

```text
JWT tokens
OpenF1 tokens
MQTT username/password
authorization headers
API credentials
```

The Agent 18.8 implementation explicitly includes secret redaction and tests against this class of leakage. fileciteturn103file0L130-L137

---

# 42. Do a final live `/metrics` secret check

Run:

```powershell
$r = Invoke-WebRequest http://127.0.0.1:8000/api/v1/metrics
$r.Content
```

# Agent 18.8.2 — Prometheus Repository Hygiene

This addresses the fact that `prometheus-3.15.0.windows-amd64` is sitting inside the RaceVision repository and could accidentally be staged by `git add .`.

The safest approach is to **prevent accidental tracking**, not silently delete the user's local executable installation. 

:::writing{variant="document" id="38152" title="Agent 18.8.2 — Prometheus Repository Hygiene — Cursor"}
You are Agent 18.8.2 for the RaceVision project.

=========================================================
MISSION
=========================================================

Fix repository hygiene related to the locally downloaded Prometheus 3.15.0 Windows binary directory:

prometheus-3.15.0.windows-amd64

The directory is currently located inside the RaceVision repository.

This creates a risk that a future:

git add .

could accidentally stage Prometheus binaries and other third-party runtime files.

This task is REPOSITORY HYGIENE ONLY.

Do NOT redesign RaceVision.
Do NOT vendor Prometheus.
Do NOT modify RaceVision monitoring implementation.
Do NOT change Prometheus metric names.
Do NOT alter Grafana dashboard behaviour.

=========================================================
FIRST: INSPECT BEFORE MODIFYING
=========================================================

Run:

git status --short

Then determine whether:

prometheus-3.15.0.windows-amd64

is:

A. untracked,
B. partially tracked,
C. fully tracked,
D. already ignored,
or
E. referenced by another repository configuration.

Also inspect:

.gitignore
.gitattributes
README files
infra/prometheus/
docs/
scripts/

Search for:

prometheus-3.15.0.windows-amd64
prometheus.exe
promtool.exe

=========================================================
REQUIRED BEHAVIOUR
=========================================================

1. The local Prometheus binary distribution must NOT be accidentally stageable as RaceVision source.

2. Prefer repository hygiene over deleting user tools.

3. Do NOT delete a directory that is already tracked without first determining exactly what Git is tracking.

4. If the directory is untracked:
   - add an appropriate narrowly scoped .gitignore rule;
   - do not add broad dangerous exclusions such as ignoring all *.exe files globally.

5. If the directory is already tracked:
   - report this clearly;
   - remove it from Git tracking only if this is clearly an accidental local tool installation and safe to do;
   - preserve the working copy when possible.

6. The repository must NOT depend on a developer's local Prometheus binary path.

7. Do not put Prometheus binaries into Git LFS or vendor them into RaceVision.

8. Keep the committed example configuration under:
   infra/prometheus/

9. Keep the actual local Prometheus installation separate from the repository conceptually.

10. Do not add developer-specific absolute paths such as:
    C:\Users\<name>\...
    %LOCALAPPDATA%\...
    to committed project files.

=========================================================
VALIDATION
=========================================================

After making changes:

1. Run:

git status --short

2. Run:

git check-ignore -v prometheus-3.15.0.windows-amd64

or the appropriate equivalent if the path no longer exists in the repo.

3. Verify the Prometheus executable is not staged.

4. Verify RaceVision source files remain unaffected.

5. Verify infra/prometheus/ still contains the intended committed configuration and alert files.

6. Run an appropriate repository status check.

7. Do NOT modify monitoring implementation merely to satisfy this hygiene check.

=========================================================
IMPORTANT
=========================================================

Do not treat the presence of Prometheus binaries as an application defect.

This is a developer-environment/repository hygiene issue.

Do not change:

RV18-0303

That defect remains OPEN until staging/production observability validation occurs.

=========================================================
FINAL REPORT
=========================================================

## User

Agent 18.8.4 — Final Report
1. STATUS
COMPLETE, tested offline. Provider metrics now go live through the app's normal live-session lifecycle. It's 95 added lines in three backend files plus one test file. Nothing is committed.

2. Existing architecture reused
Registry: the single app.state.provider_observations, created once in register_observability. No second registry.
Live-session lifecycle: the runtime's existing LiveSessionAdapter, which is Phase10Harness.live, exposed as app.state.runtime_facade.live. It's the same adapter object behind StartLiveSession/StopLiveSession, the mode controller's switch to live, and LiveSessionExecutionEngine's provider start.
Providers: the providers the caller passes in. Monitoring never builds or connects providers.
Why the adapter, not the engine: the app never constructs LiveSessionExecutionEngine and no route drives it. Wiring only the engine would have left metrics dead at runtime. Hooking the adapter makes every live start observable, including any the engine starts. I didn't add a new runtime or engine instance.
3. Lifecycle integration added
LiveSessionAdapter: optional observation_registry, plus bind_observation_registry() and failure-isolated _attach_observation/_detach_observation helpers.
register_observability: calls a new bind_live_session_observation(app.state), which binds the registry to runtime_facade.live.
Ordering: this runs in create_app after the facade is set (line 175) and before the app serves anything (line 258). It's deterministic at construction time; there's no startup race.
4. Registration behaviour
The provider is attached in start_session, before connect(), so a failed connection still shows up.
The label comes from the provider's statistics interface: official_f1_statistics gives official_f1, and openf1_statistics or stream_statistics gives openf1.
Providers with neither interface (such as memory_live) aren't registered.
ProviderObservationRegistry.register is now a no-op when the same object is registered again under the same label. That means an engine and its adapter sharing one registry produce a single registration and a single provider_attached event.
Starting while a session is active is rejected by the adapter, and the registration is unchanged.
5. Shutdown behaviour
stop_session() detaches the provider after disconnecting it, including on the already-stopped early-return path. shutdown() goes through stop_session().
Starting a new session detaches the previous provider first, so switching from Official F1 to OpenF1 leaves only openf1 registered.
Detach only removes the adapter's own object and is idempotent. The engine's existing detach call is harmless alongside it.
6. Failure isolation
Registry failure: errors in attach, detach or registry are logged by exception type and swallowed. A test with a registry that always raises shows the session starting, frames being accepted and processed, and a clean stop.
Provider failure: a provider whose connection fails is still reported (racevision_provider_state{state="failed"} = 1, connected = 0), with liveness and readiness at 1. The error text never reaches the metrics.
Statistics failure: a provider whose statistics raise shows observation_up = 0, and the providers collector stays up.
No live session: both providers show attached = 0 and readiness is 1. An offline app with no adapter still serves metrics.
Credentials: none are read or exported. Tests check that the fake JWT and the OpenF1 user, password and token are absent from the output.
7. Tests added
tests/monitoring/test_live_session_observation.py, 18 tests. They drive the real facade live_dispatcher with StartLiveSession/StopLiveSession, using the in-memory Official F1 provider and an OpenF1 stream client.

Requirement	Tests
1. Start registers
registry bound to the adapter (shared with the mode controller); start produces attached = 1, state{active} = 1
2. Shutdown detaches
stop and double stop; shutdown()
3. No duplicates
repeated start (one attach event); provider switch replaces; engine and adapter share one registration
4. Observation failure
registry that always raises; telemetry still flows
5. Provider failure observable
failed connection shows state{failed}; statistics error shows observation_up = 0
6. No session is valid
empty state healthy; memory_live not registered; offline app; label resolution (4 cases)
7. Exporter compatibility
an OpenF1 session exports the existing families with no credentials
8. Test results
New file: 18 passed.
tests/monitoring, tests/runtime, tests/providers, tests/api and tests/integration: 1813 passed, 10 skipped.
Full suite: 4507 passed, 11 skipped, 1 failed. The failure is the pre-existing test_phase17_agent_commits_present, which looks for commit e07b28d in the last 60 commits. It's unrelated.
9. Ruff result
ruff check . passes.

10. Mypy result
mypy backend: no issues in 1520 source files.

11. Files changed
backend/runtime/live/adapter.py (+40)
backend/monitoring/providers.py (+34)
backend/monitoring/app.py (+21)
tests/monitoring/test_live_session_observation.py (new)
backend/monitoring/README.md (lifecycle table; known-gaps line corrected)
docs/phase18/MasterDefectRegister.md (changelog entry)
The graphify graph was rebuilt with python -m tools.graphify build, which exited 0.

12. Remaining limitations
LiveSessionExecutionEngine is still not constructed by the app, because no route drives it. That's out of scope and would need a runtime decision. Any provider it starts through the app's adapter is observed.
Provider series exist only while a live session is running. This hasn't been exercised with a real Official F1 or OpenF1 session.
Unchanged from 18.8: Official F1 feed source timestamps aren't recorded, and duration summaries have no quantiles.
13. RV18-0303 status
Still OPEN. The code path works locally and offline, but provider metrics during a real live session in staging haven't been validated.

14. Commit recommendation
Ready to commit once you've reviewed it. The diff is small and self-contained: the six files above. I'd suggest feat(observability): observe live-session providers via runtime adapter. I haven't committed, as you asked.

# Agent 18.8.4 — **ACCEPTED**

with the explicit qualification:

**Production/live-provider validation is still outstanding.**

The implementation itself is properly completed and tested.

### Why I accept 18.8.4

The key architectural decision is correct: the agent did **not** create another runtime or force the application to construct `LiveSessionExecutionEngine`. Instead, it wired the existing `LiveSessionAdapter` into the existing single `app.state.provider_observations` registry.

That matters because the report establishes:

```text
create_app
   ↓
runtime_facade
   ↓
runtime_facade.live
   ↓
existing LiveSessionAdapter
   ↓
ProviderObservationRegistry
```

So provider observation now follows the application's **actual live-session lifecycle**, rather than depending on a caller manually registering a provider.

### The lifecycle behaviour is also correct

The report demonstrates:

```text
StartLiveSession
    ↓
provider attached
    ↓
connect()
```

Importantly, attachment occurs **before** `connect()`, so a failed provider connection is still observable.

Then:

```text
StopLiveSession
    ↓
disconnect()
    ↓
provider detached
```

and the detach operation is idempotent.

The provider-switch case was also tested:

```text
Official F1
    ↓
OpenF1
```

leaves only the active provider registered.

### Failure isolation is especially important

The report gives good evidence that observability doesn't become a dependency of the telemetry runtime:

```text
Observation registry failure
        ↓
logged/swallowed
        ↓
live session continues
        ↓
frames continue processing
```

It also tested provider failures and statistics failures independently.

That preserves one of RaceVision's most important architectural rules: **monitoring is derived/observational and must not become the canonical runtime path.**

### Test evidence is strong

You have:

```text
18 new tests                       PASS
tests/monitoring                   PASS
tests/runtime                      PASS
tests/providers                    PASS
tests/api                          PASS
tests/integration                  PASS

1813 focused/integration tests     PASS

11 skipped
1 pre-existing failure
```

plus:

```text
mypy backend                       PASS
graphify build                     PASS
```

The one full-suite failure remains the known `test_phase17_agent_commits_present` history-window issue and is explicitly reported as unrelated.

### Security handling is also correct

The tests verify that the provider observation path does not expose:

```text
Official F1 JWT
OpenF1 username
OpenF1 password
OpenF1 token
provider error contents
```

That is consistent with the observability security requirements already established in 18.8.

- no request/session/driver IDs become labels;
- collector failures remain isolated.

If Prometheus itself is available locally, confirm that normal scraping can continue during application load.

Do not turn Prometheus into the workload generator.

=========================================================
PHASE 7 — OBSERVABILITY OVERHEAD
=========================================================

Compare a suitable controlled baseline against the same workload with observability enabled.

RaceVision's monitoring should remain observational.

Measure whether enabling:

- monitoring
- provider observations
- metrics exposition
- API monitoring

introduces a meaningful regression.

Do not disable required production functionality permanently just to make a benchmark look better.

The purpose is to identify overhead, not conceal it.

=========================================================
PHASE 8 — LIVE-SESSION PERFORMANCE
=========================================================

Use the existing LiveSessionAdapter and existing deterministic/in-memory provider infrastructure.

DO NOT require a real F1 session for this benchmark.

DO NOT connect to the real Official F1 provider merely for load generation.

DO NOT connect to OpenF1 live merely for load generation unless the environment is explicitly configured for safe testing.

Start and stop controlled live sessions repeatedly.

Measure:

- start latency
- stop latency
- provider attach
- provider detach
- duplicate-registration prevention
- frame processing throughput where existing metrics support it
- lifecycle error rate
- cleanup behaviour

Include repeated session cycles.

For example:

start
process
stop
repeat

at a safe controlled rate.

Confirm that provider observation state does not accumulate across sessions.

=========================================================
PHASE 9 — PROVIDER OBSERVATION PERFORMANCE
=========================================================

Use the existing test providers.

Measure the effect of:

ProviderObservationRegistry

on:

- attach
- detach
- statistics collection
- metrics exposition

Test:

- normal provider
- provider connection failure
- provider statistics failure
- provider replacement
- repeated start/stop

Confirm failure isolation:

observability failure MUST NOT stop telemetry/runtime processing.

=========================================================
PHASE 10 — MEMORY / RESOURCE STABILITY
=========================================================

Measure memory before and after repeated workloads.

At minimum test:

- repeated API requests
- repeated WebSocket connect/disconnect
- repeated metrics scrapes
- repeated live-session start/stop
- mixed workload

Use the safest available local measurement method.

Do not claim a memory leak based solely on noisy OS memory measurements.

Look for:

- monotonic unbounded growth;
- connection-object accumulation;
- registry accumulation;
- metric-series/cardinality growth;
- task/thread accumulation;
- unreleased WebSocket state.

Where available, inspect Python-level object/resource behaviour.

Document limitations of the measurement technique.

=========================================================
PHASE 11 — SOAK TEST
=========================================================

Run a controlled sustained workload.

Prefer an initial local soak of:

10–15 minutes

or another safe duration supported by the machine.

Do not run an excessive soak that consumes the machine's resources.

The soak should combine a reasonable mixture of:

- API requests
- metrics scrapes
- WebSocket connections/activity
- controlled live-session activity

Do not continuously create thousands of new connections.

Observe:

- throughput
- errors
- latency trend
- CPU trend
- memory trend
- connection count
- metric-series count
- application stability
- log errors
- recovery after workload stops

The key goal is detecting degradation over time.

If the environment cannot safely perform a soak, mark it NOT EXECUTED with the reason.

Do not fabricate results.

=========================================================
PHASE 12 — FAILURE ISOLATION UNDER LOAD
=========================================================

Where practical, test controlled failures during a moderate workload:

- monitoring collector failure
- provider statistics failure
- provider connection failure
- database unavailable, if the environment supports it
- WebSocket client disconnects

Confirm unrelated functionality continues.

Do not perform destructive database operations.

Do not corrupt data.

=========================================================
PHASE 13 — RECOVERY
=========================================================

After load:

1. stop the load;
2. wait for connections to drain;
3. confirm the service remains healthy;
4. confirm metrics continue to work;
5. confirm WebSocket active count returns to expected baseline;
6. confirm provider observation registry has no stale registrations;
7. confirm Prometheus remains able to scrape;
8. confirm Grafana remains able to query if Grafana is available.

This is recovery from test load, NOT production disaster recovery.

=========================================================
PHASE 14 — TEST ARTIFACTS
=========================================================

Create bounded benchmark output under an appropriate test-results/artifacts directory if the repository already has one.

Do not create huge raw logs.

Prefer:

- CSV/JSON summary
- concise text report
- machine/environment information
- benchmark parameters
- result summary

Exclude:

- secrets
- tokens
- passwords
- authorization headers
- raw provider credentials
- uncontrolled telemetry dumps

Ensure generated runtime state is ignored by Git if necessary.

=========================================================
PHASE 15 — PERFORMANCE TEST CODE
=========================================================

Add deterministic automated tests where appropriate.

Do not convert the entire soak process into a fragile always-on unit test.

Separate:

A. deterministic automated regression tests

from

B. local/manual performance runs

from

C. staging/production validation

This distinction must remain explicit.

6. API
No 5xx responses, timeouts or transport errors across smoke and 2/5/10/25 concurrency. All 250 error-path requests returned 4xx.
Throughput stays around 85 requests/s at every level while the server uses one full CPU core, so the ceiling is on the server side. Latency rises with queueing: at concurrency 25, p95 was 867 ms and the slowest request about 3 s.
7. WebSocket
Real sockets connected at 2/5/10/25 clients with no failures, taking 63–235 ms to be ready. The active connection count returned to 0 every time.
The in-process fan-out delivered 30 of 30 frames to every client.
8. Metrics/Prometheus
Concurrent scrapes at 1/2/5/10/25 all returned 200 with the same 72 families and 115 series. There were no duplicate families, forbidden label keys or ID-like label values, and no token in the output.
300 unknown paths collapsed to route="unmatched".
Scraping every 250 ms added 0–11 ms to mean request latency. At the real 15 s scrape interval that works out to about 0.1% of a core.
9. Live Session
REST, real server: 25 of 25 start/stop cycles succeeded using the in-memory provider.
In-process, through the existing LiveSessionAdapter: I ran 100 cycles for each of five provider variants (normal, connect failure, statistics failure, OpenF1, unobserved). Each cycle took 10–15 ms on average, and no provider registrations were left behind.
10. Memory/Resource
Server memory (RSS): 251 MB after startup, 268 MB after warm-up, flat through the soak, and 252 MB after restart.
tracemalloc (Python allocations only): 250 provider-replacement cycles retained about 0.74 MB.
Limitation: RSS includes allocator caching, and 12 minutes can't rule out slow leaks over a race-length session.
11. Soak
12-minute soak: 59,446 requests with 0 × 5xx and 0 timeouts. Per-minute mean latency stayed at 42–48 ms and p95 at 63–75 ms. RSS stayed at 268.4–268.5 MB, and background WebSocket waves, live cycles and scrapes stayed healthy.
Unexplained failures: 4 client-side failures (about 0.007%) have no matching server-side error.
5-minute rerun: after I added error-type capture, a 25,118-request run had 0 failures of any kind.
Log volume: the server log grew by about 1.2 MB per minute at this load.
12. Failure/Recovery
A provider statistics failure under load caused no API or scrape failures.
After idle recovery, latency matched the baseline.
Graceful shutdown took 0.2 s, and the port was released.
Prometheus saw the target go down about 17 s after stop and come back up about 13 s after restart. Grafana's health check stayed OK.
13. Security
The scrape token was read from its file, never printed, and doesn't appear in any metrics output or in the logs.
No external F1 providers were contacted.
Raw artifacts are in the gitignored artifacts/phase18_9/. The server logs there contain local file paths, and none were committed.

### ✅ Failure isolation also looks good

# ⚠️ The important limitation: RV18-0607

The test uncovered a real capacity constraint:

```text
~85 requests/second
```

with one CPU core effectively saturated.

At higher concurrency, the system doesn't appear to gain throughput; instead queueing increases latency.

For example:

```text
Concurrency 25
p95 ≈ 867 ms
slowest request ≈ 3 seconds
```

There is also a significant logging cost:

```text
~1.2 MB/minute
≈ 72 MB/hour
```

at that workload.

Creating **RV18-0607 (P2)** for this is appropriate.

I would **not** try to hide or normalize this result. It is useful engineering evidence and exactly why 18.9 exists.

with no matching server-side errors.
